Tokyo's despatoh No. 12 of June 15,
1932, deals with proposals to devalue
the yen, either by ohanging the gold
mint value of the yen by legislative
aotion, or by gradual inflation of the
paper currenoy and subsequent establish-
ment of a reauced mint value.
These proposals are the result of
publicity given to the indebtedness of
the farmers which is said to amount to
about ¥ 6,000,000,000 and which cannot
possibly be paid under present condi-
tions.
A group of the majority party wished
to cut the yen to one-fifth of its
present value. Those opposed to this
measure are the leading newspapers, the
minority party, and the Finance and
Railway Ministers, who are both of the
majority party. As a result of opposi-
tion this proposal was not introduced
into the Diet in the form of a resolution.
No direot action to revalue the
yen was taken in the recent extraordinary
session

session of the Diet but there is a
large body of opinion both in the
Die and outside favoring devalua-
tion in some form. It would appear
that further attempts to devalue
the yen will be made. In the recent
session a bill was passed raising
the limit for the fiduoiary issuance
of ourrenoy by the Bank of Japan from
¥ 120,000,000 t0 ￥ 1,000,000,000.

The Secretary of State,

Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to re ort t at proposals have been
made in legislative circles (although not actually intro-
duced into the Diet) to devalue the Ja anese yen, either
by changing the gold mint value of the yen by legislative
action, or by gradual inflation of the paper currency and
subsequent establishment of a reduced mint value.
The advisability of devaluation of the yen has been
advanced during the past few days as a result of the pub-
licity given to the astounding indebtedness of the farmers
of

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
of the country.
This inicbtedness of the farmers was
generally known to students of Japanese economics, but
was not brought to the notice of the public and the poli-
ticians until recently.
The recent publicity has brought
out the fuct that t.e fur.ers huve been losing money
steadily for several years past.
Their debts now amount,
accordin to newspaper reports, to over Yen 1,Co0 per
family for some 3,500,000 farmin families, or a total of
nearly Yen ,000,000,000.
T uy are hopelessly in debt
and there is no possibility, unler conditions such as pre-
vail today, that their debts can be paid.
In Japan the
tendency is to avoid liquidation of debts if through such
liquidation the lender must take a loss.
Consequently
the borrower usually borrows more in crder to continue
operations and to pay interest on old loans, until finally
the buruen of indebtedness reaches a point where liquida-
tion for more than a small fraction of the debts becomes
impossible.
The creat mass of the farmers of Ja an and
many of the small industrialists and merchants are in this
position today, and it has become apparent that drastic
measures will be necessary to relieve the farmers, industri-
alists and merchants of their heavy burden of debt.
To
accomplish this purpose it was proposed to devalue the yen.
Moreover, it is believed in some circles that it is very
unlikely that the embargo on the exportation of gold and
the replacing of the currency on & free gold basis can be
accomplished unless the yen is devalued, the gold reserve
being believed to be insufficient to secure the currency
at its present mint par.
A group in the Seiyukai (the party which commands a
majority in the Lower House at present) proposed to remedy
the situation by drastically cutting the value of the yen
to
●

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
to one-fifth of its present value.
The yen is now fixed
at one momme (.1325 oz.) of fine gold, or equivalent to
.4985.
The proposal was to fix the yen at one-fifth
momme (.0265 oz.) of fine gold, or equivalent to .0997.
It was claimed that this would imnediately raise prices
and thereby pernit the debtors to pay off their debts.
It was pointed out that by this means France, Italy and
numerous other countries had creatly reduced this private
and public indebtedness.
a stor: of protest, however,
immediately arose againes this proposal, and it was shown
that in other countries the fall in the value of the cur-
rency had preceded the revalorizatior at a lower figure,
while the Seiyukai proposed to have the revalorization
precede the fall (the yen now being worth around .315).
It was further claimed that the Seiyukai plan would cause
an inmense flicht of capital and would throw tne financial
world into chaos.
All of the proninent newspapers, the
Minseito (the minority party in the Lower House), and
Finance Minister Takahashi and Railway Minister Mitsuchi,
themselves of the Seiyukai, joined in the opposition to
the Seiyukai proposal.
As & result of this strong onpo-
sition, the Seiyukai dropped the proposal, which was to
have been placed before the Diet in the form of a resolu-
tion, and instead accepted the Government resolution which
merely advised the"facilitatinof the circulation of money."
A smaller group in the Seiyukai advocated the devaluation
of the yen by a gradual process.
It was proposed that
the Bank of Japan buy gold gradually at the market price.
in order to increase its specie reserve, and at the same
time to have the Government inflate the currency, in
order to raise prices.
When the foreign exchange value
of the yen had fallen to a predetermined figure, because
of the inflation, the gold embargo would be removed and
the

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.96)
- 4 -
the yen held at the predeter..ined ficure.
This plan has
not aroused any great interest.
Although no direct action to revalue the yen was
taken i. the extraordinury session of the viet just ended,
there is a laree body of opinion  ot. in me biet und out-
side favorine devaluation in sone rorm. The seiyukai is
definitely in favor of aevaluatici, either by means of
lecislative action or by means or iflation of the curreney.
During the term of ofrice of te Tnukai abinet, inflution
was attempted but without success, as the laws goverming
the currency issue by the Jank of apan prevonted any con-
siderable increase of currency.
moreover, there was a
tendency for such curreney as was iusued to flow buck to
the sank of capan, inetead of etuyin in cir mlution. sen-
sequently all atteupts to ruise ricee by means of inflation
were unsuccessful. on the inposition o t cold e bargo
in December, l93l, the foreien exchan e value of the yen
dropped by about one-third, but prices remained steady in
Japan and in some cases declined somewhat, contrary to ex-
pectations. Further attempte at inflat or are now videly
advocated in order to raise prices and thereby ussist the
over-burdened far..er.
That such attempts will be made is idicated by recent
events. In the extraordinary session of the Diet just
ended a bill was passed raising the linit for the fiduciary
issuance of currency by the Bank of Japan from Yen 120,000,-
000 to Yen 1,000,000,000. This fiduciary issue represents
notes which can be issued on the security of Government
bonds, Treasury bills or comercial paper. The Bank of
Japan will therefore be able to increase its tax-free note
issue by Yen 880,000,000. As the note issue at present
fluctuates

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.95)
- 5 -
fluctuates around fen 1,000,000,000, the newly authorized
issue will increase the present issue by 85 to 90 per cent,
if full advantage is taken of the privilege.
In expluinine the lovernent co promise resolution
referred to above, advieing that meusures be taken to
"facilitate the virculutior of money', ir. Hatoyama,
Minister of ducation, is quoted in the  A Ciko IuL of
June 12, 1952, as havin stated to r. Suzuki, -resident
of the seiyukai, that by the te. "soot cireulation of
money" the Prenier meant a "thorough-goin inflation
policy".
These two factors woulu ihdicute that further at-
tempts to i flate the curency will be  ade in the near

(c) Price of Gold
894.515
On November 24 the Government  nounced that it had
increased the price at whicn it woulc purchase newly mined
gold from Yen 8.88 to Yen 9.94 per momme. It has been stated
that the reason for thus raising the price is t check the
smuggling of gold into "Manchukuo" here the Government
offers a better price than in Japan, and to bring hoarded
go1d
gold in this country out of the family stocking. It
is not expected, however, that these two purposes will
be accomplished since the price is still considerably
below the world price Sor gold.

MEMORANDUM:
DIVISION OF
COT 8
DEPA
Subject:
Recent Decline In Exchange bf Japanese
Currency.
This morning Dr. Feis asked that I attend a conference
894.515/
that he was having with Mr. Lockhead of the Treasury
Dopartment. Mr. Lockhead had a graph of the relative
fluctuations of sterling and Yen since March, 1934, which
indicated that the Yen had been definitely "pegged" to 
2
sterling; that the Yen fell in sympathy with sterling when
sterling began to fall toward the end of September; but
that beginning September 27, when the decline in sterling
was stopped, the Yen continued to fall until September 30.
The result is that today the Yen is cheaper in relation to
the Pound than it has been at any time since April, 1932.
Mr. Lockhead asked whether the Department could undertake
to request information from the Embassy at Tokyo.
I returned with Mr. Lockhead to my room, and we pre-
FILED
pared the two telegrams attached to the Embassy at Tokyo
181934
one of which is intended to be relayed to the Consulate
at Dairen.
When replies are received they should be communicated
to the Treasury Department.

It is noted that the Yen, which has apparently been
894.515/21
kept at a'definite relation to sterling for an extended
period of time, kept pace with the recent decline in the
exchange value of sterling, but that beginning September 27,
when sterling ceased to decline, the Yen continued to fall
until September 30. The present crossrate between Tokyo
and London suggests that the Yen may be fixed to sterling
at a lower rate.
Have you any reason to believe that the Japanese
Government made no repeat no effort to prevent this decline
of the Yen? The Department would be glad to receive further
information or comment in regard to the recent drop as
well as to the exchange policy of the Japanese Government.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE

DFFARTASSTSOANTTSEERETARY

1935 JUL 2 AM 9 19
May 10, 1935
894.515
Memorandum on Depreciation of Japanese Yen
DIVISION OF
COMMUNICITIONS
AND RDS
S
The Secretary:
A propos of your comment this morning on the de-
preciation of the Japanese yen, one or two qualifying
statements are perhaps necessary. It is true that the
old par value of the yen was 49.85x and that on May 3
of this year the value of the yen, in terms of the new
dollar, was 28.5x. It is thus correct to say that the
yen has depreciated relative to the dollar 42.3 percent.
Assuming no increase in yen costs in Japan, this shoula
enable, theoretically, Japan to lay down goods in our
markets at a price 42.8 lower than was the case prior
to the devaluation of the yen and of the dollar.
However, it is unquestionably true that there has
been some increase in yen costs in Japan.While wages, in
J0L 2-135.
terms of yen, have apparently remained substantially
FILED
stationary since the depreciation of the yen in 1931,
the cost of imported raw materials has increased con-
F/FG
siderably. Thus the wholesale price index for import
commodities in Japan has increased about 95 percent
from

=== Page 35 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
from 1931 to 1935. Offsetting this, however, is the
gain in the productivity of labor, notably in the tex-
tile industry, where the amount of yen wages paid per
spindle operated decreased from December l931 to Decem-
ber 1932 by a little over 10 percent. In view of the
fact that raw materials, as is well known, are such an
important part of manufacturing costs, constituting in
this country on the average about 60 percent of the
value of manufactured products, and in view of the
fact that raw materials for manufacture are in such
large measure imported into Japan, it seems certain
that the yen costs of manufacturing in Japan have in-
creased very considerably since the depreciation of the
yen in December 1931.
Offsetting this rise in costs in Japan is the rise
in costs and prices in the Unied States. This also
must be taken account of in considering the competitive
position of Japan in this market. Here, however, we
must be careful not to take the rise in costs and prices
in the United States from the low point in 1932, but
rather from December 1931 when Japan abandoned the gold
standard. Our costs and prices are at present somewhat
above the December 193l level, but probably not very
materially,

=== Page 36 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
materially, whereas Japanese costs have certainly risen
very considerably above the December 1931 level. In
view of probable greater rise of costs in Japan since
December 1931, in comparison with the rise in cost in
the United States since December 1931, it is likely
that the advantage which the Japanese have gained in
the American market is very materially less than the
42.8 percent indicated above.
Moreover, it should not be forgotten that when
Japan returned to the old parity following the War,
it is quite clear that the yen was overvalued in rela-
tion to the dollar. When this fact is also taken account
of in conjunction with the point mentioned above, it is
not unlikely that the advantage gained by Japan in our
market as a result of the depreciation of the yen at
the present time is not more than 25 or 30 percent.
A-S/T:AHH:KT

According to cables received, Japan has shipped to

894.515/29
With the latest shipment of October 19, I calculate
that Japan has left a reserve of only 1,023,000,000 yen.
This figure does not include the small amounts of newly
mined gold which may have been purohased by the Gold
Fund Special Account; neither does it include the small
gold reserves of the banks of Taiwan and Chosen pur-
chased recently by the Gold Fund Special Account, pay-
ment being made in Bank of Japan notes.
Of the Japanese gold reserve, 800,000,000 yen con-
stitutes the monetary reserve of the Bank of Japan and
F/MR
can soarcely be touched without alteration of the statutes
governing operations of this bank. There is available
shipment, therefore, gold valued at not more than
250.000,000 yen.

October 4, 1937.
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
SUGGESTED EMBARGO AGAINST JAPANESE GOLD
894515
I telephoned Mr. Archie Lochhead, exchange specialist
in the Treasury, and told him that the idea came in from
894.5
various quarters that the United States should stop pur-
chasing gold from Japan.
Mr. Lochhead said the idea was an old one and had been
30
thoroughly canvassed. From a practical point of view,
Japan would simpley get its gold to this market indirectly
instead of directly, for example, by selling in London where
it would be melted down and lose its identity, the Japanese
transferring the proceeds to the United States, or by many
different ways which Mr. Lochhead, as an exchange man, could
think of. A broader consideration is that if we start say-
ing that gold is not good in a real emergency, we shall
be destroying something in which the United States has a
greater stake than any other country. The value of gold
largely rests on the fact that it is an emergency reserve.
YILE
Destroy its use for that purpose and you destroy much of
its value.
Mr. Lochhead said that to do anything of the kind we
should
F
11

=== Page 50 === (confidence 0.97)
-2
should have an agreement with Great Britain and France.
He recalled that when the League of Nations imposed sanotions
on Italy, they did not include an embargo on the purchase of
Italian gold, and it was argued at that time that nothing
weakens a country more quickly than taking gold from it.
He did not think that other countries would come in on such
a proposal vis-à-vis Japan.
He said these ideas seemed to arise largely because
other commodities, like cotton, had friends who would resist
any interference with cotton trade. It was easier to sug-
gest treating gold as a commodity and embargo it.
He referred to a possible question of neutrality, as to
whether purchases could be suspended from one country only.
EA:FL:DJW
4

ON THE MATTER OF CEASING GOLD PURCHASES FROM JAPAN

This would be an act of discretion and policy. Pur-
chase of gold (providing dollars in return for gold shipped
89
out of Japan) could not be considered a credit transaction,
4.5
and neither private institutions nor governmental institu-

5
the neutrality act applied. Under legislation now on the
0J
statutory books, nowever, the Treasury is in a position to
refuse to make these purchase (through the reserve system)
if it shoula so decide.
Any such action, I presume, woula have for its purpose the
curtailment of the ability of the Japanese Government to ac-
quire munitions and war supplies. If taken by the American
Government alone, it would be obviously ineffective for this
purpose. The Japanese could dispose of their gold in other
places, securing other foreign currencies for gold and utilize
NV
these foreign currencies to discharge obligations.
2

its gold supplies in active external purchasing power, but I
F

commodities such as oil, it might inauce companies like the

aelivered in some distant place in payment for oil.

unless these six countries make a decision not only to
refuse to buy Japanese golu wnen delivered now directly to
them, put also announced that they voula permanently refuse
to receive that gold (on the upposition that it coula be
permanently identified), I would anticipate that Japan would
continue to find a means of utilizing its gold supplies.

NEW SUBSIDIARY CURRENCY OF JAPAN
By Imperial Ordinance No. 388, published in the
Official Gazette for June 1, 1938, it was provided that
in accordance with Articles 3 and 4 of the Emergency
Currency Law, which was passed at the last session of
the Imperial Diet, the following subsidiary coinage should
be issued:
10-sen coin
Material: Aluminium bronze.
Composition: 50 parts aluminum and 950 parts copper.
Weight: 4 grams.
Diameter: 22 millimeters.
Diameter of hole in center: 4.6 millimeters.
The design of the coin shows on one side the chrys-
anthemum seal, diverging lines emblemetic of the rising
sun, and the amount stamped in Chinese ideographs, while
on the other side the Chinese ideographs for "Japanese
Empire" and the year of issue accoruing to Japanese
chronology.
5-sen coin
Material: Aluminum bronze.
Composition: 50 parts aluminum and 950 parts copper.
Weight: 2.8 grams.
Diameter: 19 millimeters.
Diameter of hole in center: 4 millimeters.
The design of the coin shows on one side the chrys-
anthemum seal and the amount stamped in Chinese ideographs
and

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
and on the other side the Chinese iaeographs meaning
"Japanese Empire" and the year of issue according to
Japanese chronology.
1-sen coin
Material: Brass.
Composition: 900 parts copper and 100 parts zinc.
Weight: 3.75 grams.
Diameter: 23.03 millimeters.
There is no hole in the center.
The design of the coin shows on one side the chrys-
santhemum seal and the amount stamped in Chinese ideo-
graphs, while on the other siae the Chinese ideographs
meaning "Japanese Empire" and the year of issue accord-
ing to Japanese chronology.
50-sen note
Width: 65 millimeters.
Length: l05 millimeters.
Face of note: Ideographs and design at the top of
the note, black. Background of the upper pert of the
note, orange-yellow. Background of the lower part of
the note, blue. Year of issue on left boraer and amount
in the lower right corner, red.
Design of face: On the left side, a picture of
Mr. Fuji, in the upper right corner a section of the
offulgent sun, in the lower left corner a seal of the
Finance Ministry and a spray of cherry blossoms, in
the lower right corner cherry blossoms.
Back of note: ▲ conventional design printed in dark
green.
There

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.99)
-4 -
There is enclosed with this report the issue of
June 8, 1938, of the Shuho (Weekly Report), published
in Tokyo by the Intelligence Bureau of the Cabinet.
Black and white arawings of the designs of the new sub-
sidiary currency are shown on pages 26 and 27.
Applicable to colonies
In the same issue of the Official Gazette is pub-
lished Imperial Ordinance No. 387, which makes the
Emergency Currency Law effective in Chosen, Taiwan, and
Karafutc.
Purpose of change of currency
The primary purpose of the change in subsidiary
coinage is to use for other purposes the metallic nickel
which is now circulating in the form of 5-sen and 10-sen
coins, and to stop using silver for the 50-sen ccins.
Prior to September 1, 1933, the 5-sen and 1C-sen coin
consisted of 250 parts of nickel and 750 parts of a
copper alloy. After that date, they were made of pure
metallic nickel. According to the Official Gazette of
May 7, l938, the total issue of pure nickel coins since
September 1, 1933, was ¥20,500,000 in 10-sen coins and
¥7,720,000 in 5-sen coins.
Total weight of nickel in subsidiary coinage
The nickel 10-sen coin weighed 8.3333 grams and the
5-sen coin 4.l666 grams, accoruing to Imperial Ordinance
No. 232, of August 31, 1933. Some of the currency issued
since then has undoubtedly been lost, but if it could all
be

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
be recovered and replaced by the new coinage authorized
by Imperial Ordinance No. 388, of June 1, 1938, it would
amount to 2,351,649.54 kilograms.
Imports of nickel
Total imports of nickel for all purposes into Japan
from 1931 to 1936 were as follows:
Year
metric Tons
1931.
812
1932.
1,844
1933.
3,258
1934.
2,638
1935.
3,417
1936.
2,578
The figures for l937 are not available.
Comparison of coinage with inports
When it is considered that the total weight of nickel
coinage since September 1, 1933, 1s 2,352 metric tons and
that this amount has been coined in less than five years,
it is seen that of the imports since September 1, 1933, an
average of approximately 5oo tons a year has been used for
coinage.
The average importation for the last three years for
which figures are available was 2,877 metric tons annually.
If the 5oo tons used annually for coinage is deducted from
this amount, it leaves 2,377 metric tons annually for
purposes other than coinage. The nickel coinage now in
circulation thus amounts to about one year's normal
consumption

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.99)
consumption of nickel for purposes other than coinage.

LAW NO. 86.
42
TE.PORARY CURARH CY L
Article 1. When the necessity exists the Goverament may issue
subsidiary coins other than those provided for in the stipulations
of Article 3 of the currency law.
Article 2. Temporary subsidiary coins shall be of three denomin-
ations; ten sen, five sen and one sen.
Article 3. Temporary subsidiary coins of ten sen and five sen
shall be used as legal tender up to five yen, and temporary
subsidiary coins of one sen up to one yen.
Article 4. Materials, quality, weight and forms of temporary
subsidiary coins shall be specified by Imperial Ordinance.
Article 5. The Government shall, if necessary, in addition to
temporary subsidiary coins, issue a small paper money of fifty
sen denomination. The small paper money shall be used as legal
tender up to ten yen. The form of the small paper money shall
be specified by Imperial Ordinance.
Article 6.

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
Article 6. Regardi..g the amount of issue of small paper
money, the Government shall, in accordance with Government
orders to be issued, require the Bank of Japan to set aside a
corresponding amount of the overnment's deposits and to keep
it as a reserve for conversions. S all paper money mey be
converted into other currency.
Article 7. The sank of Japan shall, as deter: ined by the
Linister of Finance, handle the issue, redemption and conversion
of small paper oney.
Supple entary Rezulations.
The present Law shall be put into torce from the day of
promulgation.
The temporary subsidiary coins the the shall paper money
shall not be issued after the lanse of one yeer followin date
of the terminstion of the present Japan-China conflict.

4. Commem.
. From the manner in whioh the various categories
of gold holders are defined, it is evident that the
government contemplates that this law will ive an
accurate 1dea of the hold ings of t is metal which may
be available should the necessity for drawing on it
arise. It is also likely that this law has been passed
with a view to shedding light on gold holdings which
have not appeared as a result of the measures mentioned
in Par. 8, Roport No. 9451. There is no mention in any
press comment as to the reason for lack of speoifio mon-
tion of gold in the form of ornaments, jewelry, decora-
tions, eto., in the rovisions or this luw, und it is
thought that this ommission grows out of the fact that
the Law in question 1s designed to reach only gold in
more substantial form and whion is readily avallable.
The feet of the exemption from report of gold held in
foreim countries appears to substantiate this opinion,
although gold export restrictions in many foreign coun-
tries may have some bearing on this point. The law is
sommwhat unusual in that it states no penalties for
evasion or failure to comply wi th its provisions.
b. No great amount of information hus come to lignt
as to the amount of  old obtained through the  easures
mentioned in Par. 8, Report No. 9451. The only item
whieh has appeared in the press has been a report to
the efreet that, by the end of optember 1938, the Bank
of Formosa had bought gold to the amount of some
25,000,000, the greater portion of whion was in the
form of ormaments turned in by natives. to use this
figure as a bazis for estimates as to what may be
forthooming from the remainder of the Empire is com-
sidered as misleading, as the Japenese have never in-
dulged in gold ornamentation in the same manner that
have the mative Pormosan tribes while in a relatively
primitive state.

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
Changing Policies Regarding Gold Exports Before 1937
Japan went off gold in September, 1917, and stayed
off for 12 years. Although some provision was made
for permitting gold exports, practically a gold embargo
was put into effect.*
This embargo was removed on January 11, 1930, and
there was a great outflow of gold. Within only lt
months specie valued at l20 million yen was exported
and shipments kept on being made with both foreign and
Japanese banks playing a part.** From 308.6 million yen
in 1930 the value of gold exports increased to almost
420 million yen in 1931.**
The specie reserve of the Bank of Japan which
amounted to more than one billion yen before the embargo
was removed in January, 1930, had dwindled to about 470
million yen by the end of 1931.***
With a change in cabinet a gold export embargo
was reimposed on December 13, 1931. In 1932 and 1933
other restrictive measures were taken. Gold exports
fell to 112.7 million yen in 1932 and then to 20.9
million in 1933. During the following years there were
no more gold exports.****
Resumption
*THE JAPAN YEAR BOOK, 1935, page 355.
**See "Gold Exports" attachment to this report.
***THE JAPAN YEAR BOOK, 1935, Page 355. THE JAPAN YEAR
Book, 1933, pages 339 to 344, contains a long article on
"The Gold Embargo, Its Removal and Its Replacement."
****Exports in 1934 were valued at only 600 yen. There
were no recorded exports in 1935 and 1936. Figures for
several years from 1930 are given in the "Gold Exports"
attachment to this report.

=== Page 145 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
Resumption of Gold Shipments in 1937
In March, 1937, some months before the outbreak
of the trouble in China, the Japanese Government,
through the Yokohama Specie Bank, began to send gold
to the United States. An emergency situation was
already considered to have arisen as a result of the
need of commodities for the expansion of the productive
capacity of the Empire under a "wartime system"*, the
policy of the Government to maintain the exchange rate
of the yen at the level of 1s.2d., and the desire to
balance international payments.
Until the outbreak of the trouble in China the
general public was allowed to have information about
these gold shipments but from the latter part of the
summer of 1937 a policy of secrecy has been maintained.
Official statistics are no longer published. The fact
that shipments are being made has had to be admitted,
especially in answer to interpellations of members of
the Diet, but official statements are rarely even as
definite as the following remark made in December, 1938**,
by the Minister of Finance (Seihin Ikeda):
"Japan shipped ¥860,000,000 worth of gold
abroad in 1937, but this year's amount is
considerably less. A fairly large amount,
nevertheless, is being shipped out of the
country."
Under
*THE JAPAN YEAR BOOK, 1938-9, page 331, Column 1.
**At the 37th Annual Convention of Kwansai bankers held
in Osaka.

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6 -
Under requirements of American law gold shipments
to the United States have to be declared and as a
consequence American consular records give an insight
into the movement of gold out of Japan.
Total of Gold Shipments from March, 1937, to Middle
of February, 1939
During the comparatively brief period from March,
1937, through the middle of February, 1939, gold ship-
ments from Japan to the United States amounted to the
large total of over 13.2 million troy ounces (412.6
million grams) with a net value of over 1,608 million
yen. Almost all these gold shipments went out from
the port of Kobe.* There were no exports in January,
1938. The largest shipments were in July ana October,
1937, and in July and December, 1938.
Drain on Gold Supplies
These exports constitute a great drain on Japan's
possible gold supplies and there is naturally a desire
on the part of officials to try to conceal the true
situation. On the other hand, however, the constant
emphasis laid recently on the need of producing more
gold, mobilizing privately owned gold, and economizing
in industrial and other uses of gold reflects the
seriousness with which the Japanese Government must
regard the continuance of gold exports.
Some
*Detailed figures are given in the "Gold Exports"
attaohment to this report.

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
Some efforts are, of course, made to minimize
the possible dangers in the situation. In February,
1939, when pressed for comments on the relations
between the gold shipments and the yen exchange rate,
the Minister of Finance (Sotaro Ishiwata) is said to
have remarked that the yen exchange rate will not
decline even if the shipments of gold abroad are
continued. Gold reserves and foreign exchange are,
he said, two different problems. Although he desired
that Japan's gold reserves shoulå not decrease, he
believed that the purchase of raw materials with gold
and the export of goods manufactured with the imported
raw materials would more than balance Japan's inter-
national accounts.*
*OSAKA MAINICHI, February 5, 1939.

Profit through Revaluation of Gold Reserves
The Government's profit on the revaluation of the
gold reserves of the Banks of Japan, Taiwan and Chosen
at this time was calculated at 995 million yen.**
out of this total profit the Government paid the

turned over to a Gold Fund Special Account.
Gold Fund Special Account***
200 million yen of the Gold Fund Special Account
was to be used for the purchase of Government bonds.
The fund, up to 50 million yen, was to be available
for buying newly mined gold and for subsidizing the
gold mining industry.

Gold produced in 1937 and 1938 has apparently
already been drawn upon for exports. Officials have
admitted that the advance estimate that production in
the Japanese Empire might amount to 64 metric tons in

there have interfered with operations.

Increases in Bank Notes
The Bank of Japan is required* to hold as a
conversion reserve against the issue of notes gold
and silver** coins and bullion of the same amount as
the notes issued.
In addition the Bank can issue fiduciary bank-
notes to the amount of 1 billion yen against Govern-
ment bonds, Treasury notes and other reliable
securities or commercial bills.***
In the spring of 1938, when the Bank of Japan
had gold reserves valued at over 8ol million yen, the
untaxable fiduciary limit was raised to ¥1,700,000,000.
In spite of the reduction in the gold reserve to about
501 million yen in July, 1938, the notes in circulation
of

Inflation Fears
Since the Government adopted a policy of secrecy
in gold matters, there has been in Japan some uncertainty
as to exactly what is happening.
The press is silent, guarded in its comments or
seemingly optimistic about the subject of inflation.***
An English language publication ventured to remark
in 1938:
"One financial cushion was removed last
year through the revaluation of the gold
reserve. And a less substantial one was
created by raising the fiduciary note issue
limit."x
Interpellators in the Diet have been sometimes
outspoken in their expressions of fear about inflation
due to issues of notes and especially bonds but the
Government has tried to give them reassurances like

- 28 -
Gold Production Below Expectations
Japanese officials have admitted more than once
that Japan is in need of gold and also that gold
production has not been coming up to expectations.
Hopes of a greatly increased output in the Japanese
Empire have not been fulfilled and in "Manchukuo"
also production figures are far below those set forth
some time ago under a Five Year Plan for that country.
Typical of official expressions of uneasiness
are the following statements made by Japanese officials
in the month of January 1939:
Finance Minister Seihin Ikeda (January 1, 1939):
"The need for gold to meet international
payments has gradually become serious.
Despite the Government's efforts the
increase in gold output has failed to
reach expectations."
Mr. Kakichi Takeuchi, Vice Director of the
Material Supplies Adjustment Bureau of the
Ministry of Commerce and Industry (January 9,
1939):
"With both foreign trade and gold produc-
tion dropping off the nation must make
greater efforts than ever to conserve its
metal resources... Actually export promotion
and increased gold production are very hard
to realize under present circumstances..."
Finance Minister Sotaro Ishiwata (January 24, 1939):
"It is true that the gold production record
in this country during 1938 was not up to
earlier expectations."
Japan's Gold Production Comparatively Small
Japan is not of first rank as a producer of gold.
Countries such as the Union of South Africa, the Union
of Soviet Socialist Republics, the United States, and
Canada are much more important sources of supply of gold.*

Gold Produotion in the Japanese Empire
Statistics for gold production in Japan often
show some variance even in ordinary times and since
the outbreak of the trouble in China there are no
figures which can be relied upon.*
Total gold production in the Japanese Empire is
reported to have increased from 34 metric tons in 1935
to 41 metric tons in 1936. It appears certain that
since 1936 there have been further increases but an
estimate of 51 metric tons for 1937 may possibly be
subject to question and the preliminary estimate of
64 metric tons for 1938 can already be inferred from
official admissions to have been much too high.
Contributions of Various Parts of the Empire
From various conflicting figures** it would appear
that in 1936 the different parts of the Empire con-
tributed roughly the following quantities and values
of gold:

- 34 -
Gold Found in Many Parts of Japan Proper
Gold is found on all the large islands of Japan
and has been mined from early times on the small
island of Sado north of the main island of Honshu.
Chief Producing Centers and Producers
Hokkaido
On the northern island of Hokkaido gold is
obtained especially in the northern and in the south-
western parts. In the northwest part the Sumitomo
interests are reported to have had an output of about
lt metric tons in the Kitami area where other
companies are also operating on a smaller scale, and
at the Sanru mine in the Teshio River valley district
the Mitsui interests obtained somewhat less than
1/3 a metric ton. In the southwestern area one of
the important mines is operated at Shizukari by the
Shizukari Gold Mining Company which is said to have
had an output of more than one half a ton in 1935.
Honshu
On Honshu, the main island of Japan, gold mines
are in existence at many spots in the northern and
eastern area and as far south as the Izu peninsula.
In the central and western areas of the islanā gold
is also found and although there are fewer mines,
the output is important.
Among the most outstanding prefectures in the
amount of gold produced by mines or refineries is
Ibaraki not far to the northeast of Tokyo where the
Nippon Mining Company controlled by the Nissan
interests is reported to have produced over 3t metric
tons

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.99)
- 35 -
tons in 1935.
Another leading mining center is Ikuno in Hyogo
Prefecture in the Kobe consular district not far to
the northwest of the city of Kobe. At Ikuno mining
operations are carried on by the Mitsubishi interests.
The gold-bearing ore is taken for refining to the
small island called Naoshima* between Honshu and the
island of Shikoku. Production in 1935 is reported to
have amounted to over lt metric tons.
The Prefecture of Akita ranks as one of the most
important of the gold-producing districts in northern
Honshu. Among the producing companies may be named
especially the Mitsubishi interests, the Fujita
Mining Company, and the Dai-Nippon Mining Company.
Gold obtained by the Mitsubishi interests from
Sado island belonging to the north coast prefecture
of Niigata amounted to about 3/10 of a metric ton
in 1935.
other prefectures in the northern area of Honshu
which are particularly important in bringing up the
total gold production figures of Japan Proper are
Tochigi, Iwate, and Fukushima.
Shikoku
*Politically under the Prefecture of Kagawa on the
island of Shikoku.

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.99)
- 36 -
Shikoku*
On the island of Shikoku in the Kobe consular
district the Sumitomo interests mine gold, as well as
copper and silver ore, at their Besshi mines in Ehime
Prefecture and take the ore over to an island called
Shisakajima in the Inland Sea for refining. Production
of gold in 1935 amountea to over 8/10 of a metric ton.
Kyushu
The Prefecture of Oita near Beppu in the north-
eastern area of Kyushu outranks any other single
prefecture in Japan Proper in gold production, its
total output of mined and refined gold amounting to
more than 5 metric tons in 1935.
At a refinery of the Nippon Mining Company at
Saganoseki on a peninsula east of Beppu production
of refined gold in 1935 totaled 3.1 metric tons but
some of this gold came from sources outside of Kyushu,
including Chosen, and appears to have included some
gold from mines other than those belonging to the
Nippon Mining Company.**
In
*Production from the Mitsubishi refinery on the small

report in connection with Ikuno on the island of Honshu,
is sometimes ascribed to the Prefecture of Kagawa on
Shikoku island because Naoshime is under the prefectural
government of Kagawa.
In addition to Naoshima and Shisakajima another
small island in the Inland Sea used for gold refining
is Chigirishima. Chigirishima is under the jurisdiction

=== Page 183 === (confidence 0.99)
- 40 -
Early History of Gold Mining in Taiwan
More than 400 years ago Japanese invaders
discovered placer gold in a river gorge in the
eastern part of Taiwan (Formosa) in the district now
known as Karenko. In the years that followed gold
was found from time to time in several other places.
By 1903 the total amount of gold produced in Taiwan
amounted to more than one half metric ton. For a
while production declined but during the years 1934
and 1935 it went up to over 1 metric ton a year.
Present Producing Centers
At present the commercially important gold-
producing centers are in the extreme northeastern
part of the island in the neighborhood of the town
of Keelung (Kiirin). In this area there are two
main producers. One of these, the Tanaka Mining
Company, operates the Kinkwaseki mine and refinery,
with a reported gold production valued at more than
4 million yen in 1936. The other, the Taiyo Mining
Company, claimed to have obtained from its Zuiho mine
gold valued at more than 1 million yen in 1935 but is
reported not to have continued to have a satisfactory
output.*
Placer gold is apparently no longer important in
Taiwan, production in 1936 being valued at less than
¥160,000.**
Present

Gold the Most Important Mineral of Chosen
Gold is the most important mineral product of
Chosen.*
It is found in very many parts of the country
but some of the most profitable mines are operated in
the northwestern parts of the country in the Province
of Kokai and especially in the Province of North
Heian which borders on "Manchukuo".
Activities of Americans and Other Foreigners**
Americans have been leaders in gold mining
developments in Korea (Chosen) and some of the best-
paying gold mining property is still in the hands of
Americans.***
Americans were already interested in gold mining
as far back as 1895 and in 1896 an American obtained
a gold mining concession near Unsan. Other foreigners
thereupon began to request and obtain concessions.
In l9l6, however, there came into effect a law which
required that henceforth mining rights could be
granted only to Japanese subjects or to legal corpora-
tions organized under Japanese law. Rights previously
granted to Americans and other foreigners were,
nevertheless, still respected.

Increase of Japanese Investments in Gold Mining

The Nippon Mining Company still sends much gola

(Tinnanpo) on the western coast of northern Chosen
it is expected to be able to handle in the near future
larger and larger quantities of ores bearing gold as
well as other minerals.
The Chosen Refinery Company
has a refinery at Choko near Kunsan on the western
coast of southern Chosen. Farther inland in the
general neighborhood of Kunsan the Mitsubishi interests
are reported to be operating a large gold dredger near
Kintei.

=== Page 196 === (confidence 0.99)

"Manchukuo" Closely Connected with Japan's Gold Plans
Regarded as one of the greatest potential sources
of gold in the Far East, "Manchukuo" looms important
in Japan's plans for getting more gold.
Total Gold Production in "Manchukuo"
According to a statement of the "Manchukuo"
Ministry of Industries gold production by commercially
important companies in "Manchukuo" amounted to 3,977
kilograms in 1936.*
Types of Gold and Gold-Producing Areas
So far much of the gold obtained has been placer
or alluvial gold from the Anur Valley areas near the
northern border and from eastern Manchuria. Mountain
gold has, nevertheless, been mined at a few places in
South Manchuria and is believed to exist in a number
of other places in the country, as, for example, the
Barga and Sanho regions in northwest Manchuria south
and north of the town of Manchouli.
Estimated Manchurian Gold Resources
It is far from definitely known how much gold
Manchukuo" can produce in the future. Gold-bearing
quartz is hard to discover in many areas with wind
blown brown soil from inner Asia covering much of the
land.** The "Manchukuo" Ministry of Industries has
estimated that the gold resources of "Manchukuo" amount
to
*Information given in AMERICAN-MANCHURIAN TRADE PROSPECTS,
p. 43. See also the "Gold Production" attachment to this
report for other sources giving only 3,750 kilograms.
**Statement made by M. R. Arick, Assistant General
Manager of The Oriental Consolidated Mining Company.

=== Page 197 === (confidence 0.99)
- 53 -
to 7 billion yuan in alluvial gold and 6 million
kilograms of gold ore.*
Obstacles in the Past
In the past gold mining in Manchuria has been
hindered by many factors, such as political unrest,
lack of funds, unsatisfactory means of communication,
insufficient motive power, and shortage of mining
technicians.
Gold Production Before "Manchurian Incident"
From 1926 to 1931 gold production in the three
North Manchurian provinces of Kirin, Heilungkiang and
Hsingan is estimated to have amounted to an average
of about 1,189 kilograms a year.**
Unrest Following "ianchurian Incident"
In the years of unrest which followed the
"Manchurian incident" little gold was obtained.
Organization of Manchuria Gold L ining Company
In May 1934, the Manchuria Gold Mining Company
was organized under Japanese auspices*** and under
Japanese control, although the Government of "Manchukuo"
is reported to be represented.****

Advantages of Increased Gold Production for Japan
In urging an increase of domestic gold production
during the present trouble in China, the Japanese
Government has pointed out that it will aid national
defence, strengthen industry, improve the international
balance of trade, and help stabilize the economic life
of the people.
It has even been said by the Japanese Linistry of
Commerce and Industry that whether or not Janan can
acquire an adequate amount of gold constitutes a key
to determine whether the country will be able to cope
with the present emergency.*
Interest in New Sources
Although Chosen and purticularly "Iianchukuo" are
looked upon as the sreatest potential sources of supply
of gold, there are hopes or finding gold in other places
also. Even when a slicht trace of gold is discovered
nowadays, there is keen interest. Newspapers have
recently given publicity to the finding of traces of
gold in the Sumida River banks in Tokyo, in the
mountains just back of Kobe**, in river valleys north
of Kyoto***, in national park areas south of Nara****,
and
*THE FAR EASTERI: REVIEW, September, 1938, p. 537.
**At Yamada-mura, Luko-gun, iyogo Prefecture.
***In the Fukui and Shiga Prefectures in the valleys of

China has also been mentioned as a source of gold*
and the Japanese are evidently planning to investigate
the possibilities of getting gola from there.
Gold Encouragement Policies
Production of gola in Japan since 1932 has been
partly encouraged by reduction of railway freight rates
on gold ore, reduction of Government analysis fees, and
guidance for small scale mines.**
Gold Production Law
A special effort to foster gold production was
made through the passage of a Gold Production Law in
August, 1937, after the be innin; of the trouble in
China. This law provided for grunting subsidies***
to miners, refiners and prospectors and for relief from
import duties on necessary equipment and materials.
On the other hand, it also gave the Government nmuch
more control over the gold industry and aimed to
concentrate all newly refined gold into the hands of
the Government.****

Japan Gold Production Promotion Company*
A company called the Japan Gold Production
Encouragement Company has been formed for the purpose
of increasing gold production in Japan Proper and other
parts of the Empire. The company is to have a capital
of at least 50 million yen, the Government subscribing
half. It is to invest in and finance gold mining and
refining enterprises and factories manufacturing gold
mining and refining machinery and tools. It is also
to make surveys and calculate the value of mines.**
It is already reported to be helping companies
that are in debt. ***
In "Manchuiuo" the Manclhuria Gold Mining Company****
performs very similar functions.
Plans of the company call for the investment of
a total of ¥266,500,000 within the four years commencing
1939.

Estimated Annual Production as a Result of Encouragement
As a result of its policy of promoting gold mining
the Government hopes that by 1942 the annual production
of the Empire may amount to 13l or 135 metric tons.*
of this amount 60 tons would be produced in Japan Proper
and 75 tons in other parts of the Empire (Chosen and
Taiwan).
Dressing Plants and Refineries
To get such an increased output some years from now
the present facilities of various comnercial companies
are inadequate and it is thought that it may be neces-
sary to establish about 30 more ore dressing plants
and about 50 refineries.**
In "Manchukuo" also more plants will be needed to
increase production. One of the most important develop-
ments in recent years was the opening of a Government
gold ore refinery in kukden.***

=== Page 209 === (confidence 0.99)

Obligatory Gold Reports or Census
Towards the end of October, 1938, the Ministry
of Finance issued an ordinance requiring that all those
having Japanese or foreign gold coins and gold bullion
should report their holdings to the Government. A fine
up to ¥5oo would be imposed for not reporting or for
making false statements. This census was to begin
November 15 and last until November 20 but because
reports did not come in satisfactorily, it was extended
until November 30.
Gold Articles Sold to the Government
In January, 1939, it was announced that gold
articles sold by individuals to the Government since
the outbreak of the trouble in China up to the end of
1938 had surpassed the 100 million yen mark.* of this
amount more than 35 million had come from Taiwan.
Prospects of Compulsory Selling of Gold
In January, 1939, the Ministry of Finance went over
the lists of names of gold holders who had registered at
the time of the census in November, 1938, and sent out
emergency letters requestins the sale of gold ingots,
bars, plates, coins and other gold objects to the
Government.
Results have apparently still not been sufficiently
satisfactory and recently the Ministry of Finance is
reported to have been contemplating making it compulsory
for
*25,974 kilograms at the Government buying rate of ¥3.85
per gram or 26,525 kilograms at ¥3.77 per gram.

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.99)
- 66 -
for individuals to sell their gold holdings to the
Government either through a revision of the existing
gold laws or through a special law.
No Raise in Price
The Government buying price for gold has been
usually raised once or more each year.* For a while
this was considered a good policy because it tended to
discourage smuggling and also encouraged greater gold
mining developments. At the end of January, 1939,
however, the Minister of Finance (Sotaro Ishiwata)
stated that the Government has no intention of raising
the purchasing price of gold for the time being.
Apparently it is felt that gold mining is already
sufficiently profitable, but also it seems to be
thought that hoarded gold will be held back and not
sold to the Government as long as there may be any
prospects that the Government buying price will be raised.
Attachments
*Information about prices in given the "Gold Reserves"
attachment to this report.

PROFIT CLAIMED BY JAPANESE GOVERNMENT
ON FIRST 50 MILLION YEN GOLD SHIPMENTS
IN SPRING OF 1937.
THE ORIENTAL ECONOMIST, March, 1937.
p. 136.
Gold shipments, a question pending from the time
of the Hirota regime, have at last been started. The
present administration plans to ship some ¥50 million
gold at current value to America for present purposes,
and the first instalment of ¥l8 million in bullion
weighing about 5,O62 kilograms went forward on March 9,
followed by a second shipment of some ¥l7 million also,
in bullion and weighing about 4,700 kilograms, which
left on Maroh 15, the two making a total of about ¥35
million. When the projected ¥5o million transfer is
completed, the national treasury is scheduled to realize
a ¥5,840,000 profit, as announced at a plenary budget
session of the Diet as follows:
Profit in Gold Shipments
A. Volume exported
13,955 kg

per ¥1o0 .....

(C - D) .......
¥54,682,000

(E - B)
 5,840,000
The gold forwarded was purchased at current prices
by the Government after suspension of the gold standard,
so that it cost the State approximately ¥3 per gram,
far above the legal par of about ¥1 1/3. Even so, if
converted at the current level of dollar exchange at
$28t per ¥l00, and after setting aside various export
charges, the shipments are expected to yield the
Government a profit of ¥5.84 million. This sum was
included as a revenue item in the 1937-38 supplementary
budget submitted to the Diet on March 18.

- 2 -
RESTRICTION OF USE OF GOLD.
Finance Ministry Order effective December 28, 1937.
(As summarized in Mitsubishi Economic Research
Bureau's Monthly Circular No. 172 of February 1938.)
1. The manufacturing of gold products (other
than foil, leaf, thread, powder, liquid, manufactures
thereof and gilt articles) of over 0.37o fineness is
prohibited except in the case of (a) decorations, etc.,
made according to law, (b) articles of paramount need
for industrial and medical purposes, and (c) manufactures
for which the permission of the Ministry of Finance has
been obtained.
2. Gold foil, thread, powder and liquid cannot,
except by the Finance Minister's permission, be used
for the following purposes: (a) screens, sliding
screens, picture frames and other decorative purposes,
(b) gilt tops, lettering, binding and other similar
purposes, (c) signboards, name-plates and other
advertising purposes, (d) lettering, gilt edge, and
other printing purposes, and (e) lettering, trade-
marks and other marking purposes.
3. Manufacturers of articles in gold, gold foil,
thread, powder and liquor, who consume less than 50
grammes of fine gold per month, must submit to the
Ministry of Finance a detailed report, stating name,
address, capital, kind of manufactures and the volume
of gold consumei during each month, etc.
4. Manufacturers must report to the Ministry of
Finance the amount of gold purchased, consumed, sold,
retained and of gold manufactures produced, purchased,
sold and retained during each month.
5. The Ministry of Finance is authorized to
restrict, if necessary, the variety of gold manu-
factures or the volume of gold consumed by manufacturers.

Between March ... and the end of July gold shipments
aggregated 84,279,930 grams, this year's exports thus
exceeding the purchases by 59,020, 086 grams. To that
extent the Bank of Japan's gold reserves have been cut
down. Nevertheless, between April of 1934, when the
Bank of Japan Gold Purchase Law came into force, and
the end of this July the central bank was able to buy
1l7,71l kilograms of gold, and so, as compared with
the time before the enforcement of the gold purchase
law, its gold reserve has as good as increased by
58,691 kilograms.
*Consular note:
The foregoing figures for shipments from March to
June are in agreement with the Kobe consular records,
exactly in the case of grams and essentially so in the
case of the yen values.
However, the above figures for shipments in July,
1937, both in grams and in yen are much below the
figures in the Kobe consular records which are
51,242,218.34 grams and ¥197,751,869.26. (Figures
for shipments on different dates in July and August,
1937, as shown in the Kobe consular records are given
in the "Gold Exports" attachment to this report.)
As mentioned on page 5 of the body of this report
the round figure ¥860,000,000 was named by the Minister
of Finance in December, 1938, as the value of the gold
shipments during the whole year 1937. This figure,
which possibly takes into consideration certain shipping
expenses, is also not in disagreement with the figure
of about 852 million yen shown in the Kobe consular
records as the net value of 1937 gold shipments.

REVALUATION OF JAPAN'S GOLD RESERVES
AT END OF AUGUST, 1937.
Kilogram Quantity Analysis in TIE JAPAN ADVERTISER
ANNUAL REVIEW, 1937-1938, p. 6.
Aotual weight of gold in a Japanese gola coin is
750 milligrams to the yen, with the coin value of ¥5
per momme (3.75 grams). The bank's reserve, both in
coin and bullion, was previously carried on the books
at this value (¥5 per momme), regardless of its worth
in the world market. This practice left open the
possibility of raising the legal value at any time at
which it was considered desirable to obtain a large
amount of money on a thoroughly sound basis. It really
amounted to postponing the taking of profits accruing
from an increase in the market price of gold.
In August, a little more than a month after the
China incident began, the Diet lowered the gold content
of the yen to 290 miiligrams, which raised the legal
value of the reserve by slightly more than two-and-a-
half times, or to ¥12.93 per momme. The value was
deliberately kept approximately l0 per cent below the
market price then quoted.
On August 24, 1957, the day before this book-
keeping operation took place, the reserve of the Bank
of Japan was valued at 446,328,000, which at ¥5 per
momme amounted to 344,74o kilograms (334 metric tons,
plus).
On August 25, this weight of gold was valued at
¥1,154,204,000. 0f this am0unt, 801,000,000 (232,308
kilograms) was left in the Bank of Japan as the specie
reserve against the currency. The operation freed for
the Government's general purposes 353,204,000 worth of
gold (102,437 kilograms).
The Bank of Chosen and the Bank of Taiwan were
instructed to send their gold reserves to Tokyo,
replacing it in their accounts with Japanese currency.
On August 24, the reserve of the Bank of Chosen was
¥2,871,000 (2,153 kilograms at 5 per momme) and that
of the Bank of Taiwan was ¥15,885,000 (11,913 kilograms).
The amount available from these sources at that time was
14,066 kilograms.
The Government appears, then, to have entered the
period of secrecy concerning most of its gold dealings
with 232,308 kilograms in its currency reserve and
1l6,503 kilograms available for other uses.

=== Page 242 === (confidence 0.99)
GOVERNMENT'S PROFIT ON REVALUATION
THE JAPAN ADVERTISER ANNUAL REVIEW, 1937-1938. P. 6.
Calculation of the Government's profit on the
revaluation operation has nothing to do with the amount
of gold on hand on August 24, 1937. First taken into
account was the value of the gold in the Bank of Japan's
reserve in April, 1934. This gold originally was carried
on the books at 425,069,000 (at 750 milligrams to the
yen, or ¥5 per momme). It was revalued at 290 milligrams
to the yen, or ¥12.93 per momme, which made it worth
¥1,099,324,000. The difference between the original
and the new figures represented the book profit on the
transaction, which was ¥674,254,610.
Next, the Government took into account the amounts
of gold bought by the Bank of Japan after April, 1934.
Considering the prices at which it was bought and other
factors, the Government calculated the profit on the
purchases during the more than three years at ¥291,387,000.
Profit from these two operations was ¥965,641,000,
which is the amount turned over to the Government by
the Bank of Japan in currency.
Since the Bank of Taiwan and the Bank of Chosen
had not been involved the Government's purchases of
gold in preceding years, the profit taken fron their
reserves was based on the revaluation of the stocks
on hand on August 24. Amount obtained from the Bank of
Taiwan was ¥25,197,000 (profit on 11,913 kilograms) and
from the Bank of Chosen 4,537,000 (profit on 2,153
kilograms), a total of £29,734,000.
Combined profit from the Bank of Japan's 1934
reserve, subsequent purchases and the banks of Chosen
and Taiwan was ¥995,375,000.
From this total profit on the whole revaluation
process, the Government paid to the Bank of Japan for
gold purchased the sum of 226,162,000. It also paid
the bank ¥22, 000,000 on a debt which had been out-
standing since the bank was founded. These two pay-
ments to the bank, amounting to ¥248,162,000, were
paid out of the profits of revaluation.
Deduction of those payments to the Bank of Japan
made the net profit to the Government ¥747,249,000.
This amount, in currency or its book-entry equivalent,
was set up in a newly established Gold Fund Special
Account. At the time the account was created (August,
1937), it was stipulated that ¥200,000,000 should be
ear-marked for the purchase of Government bonds. The
remainder was to be devoted to the purchase of mined
gold and to subsidizing the gold mining industry.
The

=== Page 243 === (confidence 0.98)
-10
Most important results of the revaluation were
the increase in the note issue limit, establishment
of a new fund for the purchase of Government bonds
and the freeing of 102 metric tons of gold for
Goverrment use.

=== Page 245 === (confidence 0.99)

REVISION OF GOLD PURCHASE LAW IN 1937.
Synopsis from the JAPAN TRADE GUIDE, 1939, page 617.
The Gold Purchase Law which has been in force
since April 7, 1934, originally provided that the
Government should indemnify the Bank of Japan, up to
the maximum limit of lo0 million yen, for any loss
which may be incurred due to discrepancy between
the buying prices and the statutory parity gold price.
In view of the increased acquisitions of gold by the
Bank of Japan, however, the limit of compensation was
increased to 2o0 million yen by a revision of the law
in March, 1935. This maximum limit of indemnification
is further increased to 400 million yen by the present
revision. The revised law was promulgated on March 31,
1937, and went into force inmediately. Up to the date
of the promulgation of the revised law, the Bank of
Japan has received 179 million yen fron the Government
to cover losses arising from the gold buying operations.

=== Page 246 === (confidence 0.98)
● 13 -
GOLD RESERVE VALUATION LAW
MITSUBISHI ECONOMIC RESEARCH BUREAU MONTHLY CIRCULAR
NO. 167, SEPTEMBER 1937.
p. 18.
This law requires the Bank of Japan to revalue
its holding of gold coins and bullion constituting
the reserve against the note circulation at the new
rate of ¥1.00 per 290 mg. (¥1.333 per gram) of pure
gold, notwithstanding Article 1 of the Coinage Law by
which the rate was fixed at 1.00 per 750 mg. (¥3.448
per gram) of pure gold (Art. 1). The new rate is also
applied to the gold holdings of the Bank of Chosen and
the Bank of Taiwan. Profits gained by these three
banks through the revaluation shall be transferred to
the account of the Government (Art. 2).
It is estimated that the Bank of Japan will have
about 450 million yen (old parity) in the vaults (488
million yen at the end of July) when the law is put
into force, which, on the basis of the revised rate,
will yield a profit of 717.5 million yen after deduc-
tion of 248 million yen which the Govermment owes to
the Bank of Japan. The Bank of Chosen and the Bank
of Taiwan will contribute 4.5 million and 25.2 million
yen respectively, making a total of 747 million yen
for the three banks.

=== Page 247 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
GOLD FUND SPECIAL ACCOUNT LAW
MITSUBISHI ECONOMIC RESEARCH BUREAU LHONTHLY CIRCULAR
NO. 167, SEPTEMBER 1937.
p. 18.
This law enables the Government to establish a
gold fund as a self-balancing account (Article 1).
The fund is financed with the sum of 747 million yen
which is the net profit resulting from the application
of the Gold Reserve Revaluation Law (article 2).
The object of this law is to provide funds for
gold shipment by the Government to maintain the balance
of international payments and to safeguaru the inter-
national position of the yen. This will emancipate
the Bank of Japan in the future from the task of
shipping gold abroad on its own account. The fund
will also be available up to 50 million yen for the
encouragement of gold mining and tle purchase of newly
mined gold (Article 3). The Bank of Japan will in the
future have no connection with the purchase of cold.
This accounts for the repeal or the Bank of Japan Gold
Purchase Law.
This law also authorizes the Government to invest
in Government bonds (Article 4). This will help the
Government to finance emergency expenditure through
this channel.

INFLATION IN JAPAN
Comments by Toshie Obama, Executive Editor of the
CHUGAI SHOGYO, translated from the February, 1939,
1ssue of the NIPPON HYORON by CONTEMPORARY OPINION
as published in THE JAPAN TIMES of February 6, 1939.
NOTES CIRCULATING
In point of fact, inflation ... is already under
way in this country. The bank notes circulating in
Japan Proper and colonies at the enu of 1938 amounted
to 3,119,000,000, or an expansion of 1,062,000,000
or 50 per cent over the corresponding figure for 1936,
and of ¥636,000,000 or 25 per cent over 1937. These
increases cannot be overlooked. The following table
shows the notes circulation at the end of the past
three years (in 1,000,000):

But the numerical expansion of the note circulation
as shown in the above table must be accepted with a
grain of salt. In the first place, since the note
circulation per capita as of December 31, 1936, was
approximately ¥25, the note circulation as of December
31 1ast year might show an inflation of 50,000,000
without any evil effects, provided that the population
here increased by one million a year in the meantime.
Secondly, the 70,000,000 increase of the Government
notes, being attributable mainly to the withdrawal of
50-sen coins in exchange for small notes of the same
denomination, cannot be considered as actual inflation.
From these two considerations alone, the currency in-
flation above referred to would be reduced by some
100,000,000, and the rates of expansion would be
lowered to 40 and 20 per cent respectively as compared
with the end of 1936 and 1937.
CENTRAL

=== Page 249 === (confidence 0.99)
-16-
CENTRAL BANK'S PART
Mention must be made also of the fact that the
ourrency circulation does not increase and decrease
in a mechanical manner but can be controlled to no
small degree by manipulations on the part of the
central bank, and that accordingly it can be
prevented from being inflated seriously or it can be
deflated at a strike, if need be, after it is left
to increase for some time. For instance, the note
issues of the Bank of Japan were expanded to the
extent of 2,858,000,000 as of December 31 1ast, and
they were withdrawn by as much as 634,000,000 or 22
per cent in the first ten days of the new year. Be
that as it may, the currency inflation as it now
stands is not so serious a matter as some pessimistic
observers would put it.
Let us now approach the question of inflation
from other angles than currency. There are some people
who fear that the enormous increase of war expenditures
will lead Japan's finance to collapse. They complain
that the China Incident special account of 5,O00,000,000
for 1938-39 is abnormally large by the side of the
3,000,000,000 general budget. But tuis is not at all
a fair comparison. Includin some 40 special accounts,
the total budget estimates (exclusive of those directly
for the China Incident) were 5,326,000,000 in 1937-38
and increased to 6,500,000,000 in 1938-59. Besides,
local finances are estimated ut 1,500,C00,000, exclud-
ing those portions which are duplicated in State
finance. All told the budget estimates not directly
relating to the current hostilities would total
¥8,000,o00,O00, of which figure the Incident budget
of ¥5,000,000,000 accounts for no more than 60 per cent.
A study of bond assimilation in the past, too,
indicates that our State finance, despite its recent
expansion, is not going its course toward catastrophe.
In the calendar year of 1938, national bonds were issued
to the extent of 4,330,000,000 (3,680,000,000 under-
written by the Bank of Japan and ¥650,000,000 by the
Deposit Bureau of the Finance Ministry). Of the total
issuance, some 3,650,000,000 or 84.3 per cent were
digested, including ¥2,542,000,000 sold to the public
by the Bank of Japan, 459,000,000 sold through post
offices, and ¥650,000,O00 taken over by the Deposit
Bureau. Vice-Governor Tsushima of the Bank of Japan,
before the recent meeting of directors of the bank,
said in part: "In the seven-day period from January
4th to 10th this year. ¥134,000,000 and 216,000,000
worth respectively of national bonds and rice bilis
were sold on the market. The total absorption of
350,000,000 shows a gain of 130,000,000 over the
like period of 1938.

COMMENTS ON INTACTNESS OF
CURRENCY RESERVE
THE ORIENTAL ECONOMIST, August, 1938.
pp. 482 and 483.
CURRENCY RESERVE REMAINS IINTACT
However that may be, The Oriental Economist firmly
believes that any such suspicion regarding the Bank of
Japan's reserve is wholly unfounded. Since the outbreak
of the China conflict a year ago the Government has with-
held all information on the export or import of gold and
silver, and outside people have absolutely no means of
obtaining correct figures on the subject. Nevertheless
some facts are clear. For one thing, it is certain that
during last year fairly large amounts of old were
exported from what was held under the special gold fund
account, that is to say, the state owned gold other than
that heid by the central bank; but these exports did not
by any means represent all of the holdings under that
account. It is clear that a respectable anount of gold
remained and was carried forward to the current year.
The export of gold for last year was presumably con-
cluded before the end of Noveaber, (although the exports
for November probably appear for December in the sta-
tistical retuins of the importing country), and December
and the following months of the current year each ac-
counted for newly produced sold which, necdless to say,
was available for export. If the monihly output of gold
is assumed to be l5 million, which is a very conservative
figure, then there had been a production of i90 million
in value over a period of six montns. In addition,
Manchuria was also producing the metal. hen all these
figures are taken into acccunt, it should not be surprising
if Japan's exports of gold amounted to 17e or even 200
million by the end of May or June. Anyone who sees such
figures as those revealed in foreign statistics ana
suspects that the Central Bank was guilty of the secret
exportation of gold, can be excused only on the ground
that the Government prohibits the publication of any
statistical data on these subjects; but, nevertheless,
his ignorance of the economic conditions of this country
would be rather astonishing.

=== Page 251 === (confidence 0.99)
-18-
THREE HUNDRED MILLION
FROM SPECIE RESERVE SET ASIDE IN 1938
FOR FOREIGN EXCHANGE FUND
THE ORIENTAL ECONOMIST, AuguSt, 1938.
p. 481.
FOREIGN EXCHANGE FUND SET UP
The Ministry of Finance announced on July 19 that
it would set aside 300 million from the specie reserve
of 8o0 million held by the Bank of Japan and set up a
foreign exchange account at the bank. This was done
the same day. ... The main provisions of the arrange-
ment are (1) the specie transferred to the new fund
will be successively converted into foreign currency
holdings as occasion requires; (2) the foreign currency
belonging to this fund will be placed at the disposal
of the Yokohama Specie and other exchange banks and
used to import raw materials for the export industries,
but whatever amounts are thus used shall be returned to
the fund within a definite period by the respective
banks, who will repay the fund from the proceeds of
the export exchange transactions in each instance;
(3) therefore the fund is to be actively operated as
a revolving fund for the importation of raw materials
for export goods.

=== Page 252 === (confidence 0.99)
-19
VITALIZATION OF GOLD POLICY
Editorial in THE OSAKA MAINICHI of January 31, 1939.
We are told that as a means of concentrating all
gold and gold objects within the nation at the disposal
of the state, the Finance Office is speeding up the
drafting of a bill for the revision of the existing
gold production act with a view to introducing it in
the current session of the Diet. The revision aims at
authorizing the government to make compulsory purchases
of gold coins, gold bullion, and gold articles.
Under the protracted hostilities, we must obtain
a smooth supply of materials that are necessary for the
ammunition and expansion of the productive capacity.
Moreover, in order to maintain the exchange standard
and sound international accounts, while increasing
our purchasing power abroad, it is hardly necessary to
say that the nation must immediately provide means
positively to increase the gold output of the country
and to place all the gold at the disposal of the
government, besides strengthening the diverse export
development measures and the exchange control.
In view of the importance of gold in wartime, the
government authorities have, of course, inaugurated
numerous facilities for encouraging the gold output
increase and for collecting gold. Not long ago, they
established the five year gold output increase plan.
Accordingly they have raised the purchase price of
gold several times to increase the amount of our gold
output within and without up to ¥5o0-million a year by
1942. The price has been raised up to the international
standard. Thus the government is greatly encouraging
the increase of the gold production by the people.
On top of that, by promulgating the law for the
Japan Gold Output Development Co., the government is
extending all necessary support, namely, the technical
guidance and supply of low interest fund to medium and
small gold producers who are short of funds and tech-
nical training--all for the sake of increasing the
gold output.
When the gold output increase plan encountered a
setback to some extent owing to the rise in the cost
of production, the shortage in the supply of labor,
and the diffioulty in distribution of materials for
production, resulting from the soaring of the com-
modity prices since last year, the government,
dispatching its engineers to gold mines in all parts
of the country, conducted minute investigations on
the spot. Based on the findings of these engineers,
the authorities formulated a gold output increase
plan suitable and feasible at each particular mine.
Simultaneously, the government carried out a
preferential

=== Page 253 === (confidence 0.99)
-20-
preferential distribution of machinery, gasoline,
and other necessary materials to them, while
facilitating the smooth transportation of their
gold output and materials. Thanks to these timely
encouragements, the gold output increase plan has
come to work out favorably again, raisin the
prospects for realization of the 500-million annual
output plan. This is indeed a cause for felicitation.
On the part of gold mine operators, too, it is in-
cumbent that they collaborate with thi: national
policy now and endeovor to in cretme thcir production
by appropriate processes such as borin" and others.
As the result of the enactment of the old output
legislation, the covernment made it compulsory for all
the old mine operators to cel. their whole output to
the Bank of Japan. By thut the muthorities tried to
prevent the precious metal from being scattered and
lost or smuggled out. In keepin; with these measures,
the government, intensifyin: the restrictions on the
use of gold in entirety, tabooed the manufacturing of
any gold article without the pernission of the Finance
office save for medical instruments. By these means,
the government has attempted to concentrate gold
holdings by the state.
Since there were no accurate fi jures on the amount
of our people's sold possessions in the ubsence of a
gold national census, the government asked the people
to report their possessions of old coins and gold
bullion to the government. It is reporteo that the
government intends to conduct a nationwide investiga-
tion regarding all gold articles held by the people
before long.
Needless to say, irrespective of whether or not
a nation maintains the old standard, she must eventual-
ly settle her international accounts with gold. And so
long as her international balance is not ameliorated,
turning into an excess of receipts, the ban on the
export of gold doesn't help the situation much. Because,
for the excess of payment, that nation must either
provide her cold fund abroud or ship gold from the
country to settle the adverse balance.
The equilibrium of our international accounts is
extremely vital as the axis of our economic policy
under the emergency. Although the government is doing
everything possible to this end, it shouldn't confine
its purpose to the balancing of international accounts
alone. Since the gold policy is aimed at assuring the
supply of materials necessary for munitions and for
expanding the productive facilities, we must declare
that it is most important to provide measures for con-
oentrating the people's gold possessions into the
government or the Bank of Japan so that the nation may
increase her purchasing power abroad by repleting the
fund for the settlement of the international accounts.
Through

=== Page 254 === (confidence 0.97)
-21-
Through various facilities brought about by the
government and the gold selling campaign by the Osaka
Mainichi and Tokyo Nichi Nichi, the people's compre-
hension of cold has been promoted considerably. As
the result, many are rushing to either donate or sell
their gold and old artieles to the government and
exercisin: a strict economy in usin: gole, much to
our pleasure.
At present, tnt forei n exchane control act
empowers the covernment to make compulsory purchase
of any gold coins. To extend the covernment's authority
in this connection to all the old articles vithin the
nation, whenever the overnment finds it necessary.
must be called quite a necess ry action auidet the
emergency today.
As long as gold is hoarded in the country as
individual property, the precious metal doesn't help
a bit in improving our international accounts and
maintaining the international exchunge level. how that
the government authorities have declared in the Diet
that they will absolutel. never raise th, pureiusing
price of gold in the future, it doesn't serve any
worthy purpose for un indiviaual to kee olc und cole
articles.Such possessin is really meaningless.
Without awaitin the overment's intensifiention of
the gold policy to suel on extent as the compulsory
purchase of gole und gcld articles in general, the
people should refrain from hoarain; them. e believe
the people should voluntarily collaborute with the
government's cold concentration policy by either
selling or donatin their possessions to the govern-
ment.
