=== Page 298 === (confidence 0.99)
- 1 -
GOLD PRODUCTION LAW
THE FAR EASTERN REVIEW, September, 1938. pp. 337 and 338.
Essential Points of Gold Production Law
(Law No. 59, which went into effect August 28, 1937)
(1) Persons desiring to engage in the business
of goid refining and of buying minerals containing gold
are required to secure licences from the Government.
(2) Persons who have acquired minerals contain-
ing gold or alluvial gold or any substance containing
gold are required to refine them into gold bullion and
to sell the latter to the Government, or to sell them
to licensed gold refiners or buyers of minerals con-
taining gold. When these buyers have refined the
bullion, they are obliged to sell it to the Government.
Thus, all newly refined gold is to be concentrated by
the Government, irrespective of the channels through
which it may be obtained.
(3) Gold refiners are required to formulate
their plans of operation in advance and to report them
to the Government. Reports are also required concern-
ing changes in plans. The Government is autiorized to
order alterations in the plans of the refiners.
(4) Whenever it is deemed necessary for the
augmentation of gold production, the Government may,
on the recommendation of the Gola Commission, command
gold refiners to expand or improve refining facilities
and may give necessary instructions concerning such
facilities.
(5) Owners of mining rights and rights on alluvial
minerals containing gold shall be treated in the same
manner as gold refiners, with respect to business
operations.
(6) When it is deemed necessary for the public
welfare, the Government may, on the recomendation of
the Gold Commission, issue orders to persons who are
engaged in gold mining, gold refining and buying of
minerals containing gold.
(7) The Government is authorized to demand reports
from goid miners, gold refiners and persons engaged in
the business of buying minerals containing gold con-
cerning their business methods and assets, and may also
investigate such matters. The Government may further
issue supervisory orders regarding the accounting
methods of the above persons or may take necessary
action.
(8)

=== Page 299 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
(8) When it is deemed necessary, the Government
may, on the recommendation of the Gold Commission,
issue orders regarding the price of gold, restrictions
on the use of gold and other matters relating to the
consumption of gold. This provision has been put into
force through Ordinance No. 60 Concerning Regulations
on Gold Consumption of the Department of Finance,
promulgated on December 28, 1937.
(9) Machinery, tools or other materials necessary
in the business of gold miners or gold refiners, when
imported with the approval of the Government, shall be
exempted from import duties for a period of five years
from the day on which the Gold Production Law went into
effect.
(10) The Government may give subsidies to gold
miners or gold refiners within the limits of the amount
fixed in the budget.
The JAPAN TRADE GUIDE, 1939, contains on page 72
a very brief synopsis of the law and on page 15 a
longer synopsis approximating a translation but not
essentially different fro. the information given above.

Announoed Polioy of Retaining Gold Reserves
In April 1939, the Minister of Finance stated that
although consideration might later be given to releasing
a part of the gold specie reserves, he believed that it
would be wise for the Government not to give up its
gold, because "a protracted war requires considerable
money." He thought that even if Japan followed Germany's
policies in regard to foreign trade and restrictions on
commodity consumption, it would be better off while
retaining its gold specie.
He erpressed these opinions when industrial and
shipping leaders in Osaka urged him to release the
gold reserves and depend on newly mined gold for the
Tendenoy of Exports to Exceed Gold Production
The Finance Minister, if he had wished, could have
pointed out that the Government had already drawn
gold production for Japan's exports.

Possible Origin of Gold Exports
In May the Minister of Finance explicitly declared
that an economist who said that Japan would have no
specie reserve within the coming two months was in
utter ignorance of the situation, because there would
3
be no such development.
There is no special reason to doubt the Minister's
strong assertion. It was necessary to draw on gold
reserves in 1937 and 1939, when exports were very large,
but shipments are now going out on a very reduced scale.
Although, even with increased production in the Japanese
Empire and "Manchukuo", newly mined gold may still be
insufficient to cover exports, this does not mean that
gold reserves will have to be drawn upon to the point
of exhaustion.
Exports
1. At a Bankers' Club dinner in Tokyo. (THE JAPAN TIMES
of May 26, 1939.) To export newly mined gold at the
rate of 8.9 metric tons a month, the Japanese Empire
would have to produce, for exports only, at least
2.6 times the total quantity produced for all purposes
in 1936.
2.
60 tons in Japan Proper and 75 tons in colonial
territories. (THE JAPAN TRADE GUIDE, 1939, page 72.
A Chugai report in THE JAPAN ADVERTISER of August 14,
1938, gave only 131 tons as the aim of the 5 year gold
encouragement program.) The 5 year plan for "Manchukuo"
envisaged M14.8 million yen in 1937 but production
turned out to be only M12 million. The goal for l941
was to be Mr82 million. At one time the aim for 1939
was 36 million yen, later reduced to 25 million yen
(ac0ording to THE JAPAN TIMES of January 26, 1939).
3. At a Bankers' Club dinner in Tokyo (THE JAPAN TIMES
of May 26). (See an attachment to this report.)

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
Exports may possibly continue for a rather long
time at the present reduced rate of 8.9 tons a month
by use of gold of three different categories: (1)
newly mined gold; (2) privately owned gold sold or
donated by individuals; and possibly (3) gold hitherto
held in the form of bars as bank reserves and later
replaced by gold in other forms.
There is no proof that use is being made of gold
in the third category but there is at least a slight
possibility that with the passing of time reserves held
against bank note issues will not consist solely of gold
bars. Many gold articles sold or donated by individuals
are undoubtedly turned into gold bars for exportation.
On the other hand, there are indications that some gold
is not immediately sold or donated but is merely
entrusted to the Government by patriotic Japanese.
As an example, on March 7, 1939, the Emperor of Japan
entrusted to the Bank of Japan ancient Japanese gold
coins and other gold objects "in connection with the

Lack of Information about Use of Exported Gold
When the Government took in July 1938, the sum of
300 million yen worth of gold specie reserves from the
Bank of Japan, it said that it was establishing a
Foreign Exchange Fund to be converted into foreign
currency as needed to import raw materials and that
because amounts advanced were later to be returned,
the fund was regarded as a revolving fund for imports
of this kind. Japanese writers on economic subjects
have recently mentioned their lack of information in
regard to the exact present status of this fund.
Also no information is published as to what
happens to the gold which the Japanese Government
continues to ship to branch of the Yokohama Specie Bank
in San Francisco.
Excess of Imports over Exports
From the standpoint of foreign exchange Japan is
much more interested nowadays in its merchandise trade
with countries outside the "Manchukuo" and China area
and it is therefore interesting to note that in such
trade its recorded imports during the first four months
1
of 1939 exoeeded exports by more than 260 million yen.
Some Kobe business men believe that there are in
addition unrecorded imports for Government use.
1. An attaohment to this report gives more details.

FINANCE MINISTER'S REMARKS IN MAY, 1939,
ON GOLD, NOTE AND BOND SITUATIÓN.
(From the JAPAN TIMES AND MAIL, May 26, 1939.)
LOW YEN IN N. CHINA TEMPORARY
Ishiwata Explains Situation
To Bankers, Saying It Is
Natural in Early Stage
Refuting apprehensive views entertained in different
sections regarding the North China currency situation
wherein the Federal Reserve Bank note is underrated as
against the old legal currency of the Nationalist regime,
Finance Minister Sotaro Ishiwata declared that there is
no need of grave concern over the situation, at a dinner
party given by the Bankers' Club here Wednesday.
The Finance Minister explained the currency develop-
ments in North China from a broader perspective and
emphasized that the Federal Reserve Bank note is receiv-
ing a merely temporary compact during an early stage of
its growth. Mr. Ishiwata stated:
Temporary Nature
"The recent decline in the value of the Federal
Reserve Bank note against the old legal tender is to be
considered separately from the advancing tendency of
commodity prices in North China which has resulted in a
tight supply of goods. Owing to the fact that the
ciroulation of the old legal tender has been totally
prohibited since March l0 of this year, those who intend
to acquire the old legal tender have been selling the
Federal Reserve Bank note at unfairly low rates, and
this operation has been conducted from the international
settlement of Tientsin.
"It is of course necessary to adopt counter-measures,
but the question cannot be of serious concern. I con-
fidently believe that the foundation of the Federal
Reserve Bank note will be solidified steadily hereafter
along with the replenishment of foreign ourrency and the
freer exchange of goods between North China and Japan."
With respect to bond and monetary questions, the
Finance Minister flatly denied the recent rumor that
the Government would come out with a system to have the
people buy and hold national bonds on a compulsory basis.
The Finanoe Minister's statement in this connection may
be sumarized as follows:
"A view is being entertained by some that the
Government must set up a system to have the people buy
and hold national bonds on a oompulsory basis, but I
oannot

=== Page 326 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
cannot agree with such opinion. The Government has
no idea of establishing such a system. Based on the
examples in Germany and Italy, no favorable result
can be expected from such a system. I am of the belief
that a compulsory system would defeat its own end, and
that it would be far better and effective to accelerate
the assimilation of bonds on the basis of greater
confidence of the people on national bonds and aggres-
sive campaign for the encouragement of thrift and
savings.
Astonished
"A few days ago, a person who is considered an
authority on economics in Japan would have no specie
reserve within the coming two months, and I was astonished
at his utter ignorance of the real situation. I explicit-
ly declare that there would be no such development as
pointed out by the economist.
"Gold production in this country during the first
several months of the currency year has not shown great
increase in comparison with the corresponding period of
last year, but the Government is endeavoring in improv-
ing the situation, and it is believed that the produc-
tion for the year as a whole will show an increase as
planned. On the other hand, the measures to concentrate
hoarded gold were operated with very favorable results
during last year, and further successful results are
expected in this year. In Taiwan, it is estimated that
gold purchases by authorities on the island for April
and May would reach ¥25,000,000.
"Some people argue that the Government should raise
its buying price of gold in order to attract gold from
Manchoukuo and China, but it is quite imaginable that
should the official buying price of gold is increased
here, the gold prices in China and Manchoukuo would go
up further. Perhaps, some measures would be necessary
regarding gold in Manchoukuo and China, however, the
Government does not intend to raise its buying price
at the present moment."

=== Page 327 === (confidence 0.99)
COMMENTS OF MINISTER OF FINANCE ON GOLD
(A report of THE OSAKA MAINICHI on April 11, 1939, of
a roundtable parley in Osaka attended by Sotaro
Ishiwata, Minister of Finance, Shozo Murata, President
of Osaka Syosen Kaisya, and Shingo Tsuda, President of
the Kanegafuchi Spinning Company.)
Murata: Personally, I would much like to dwell on
the question of expansion of Japanese shipping, with
which I am connected, but because it would entail too
muoh time, I shall pass over that subject today. Since
the Sino-Japanese confliot has entered a new stage, I
think we must all tighten our grip and assume a posi-
tive stand at every turn.
In view of that fact, I would suggest that the
government now release the specie reserve it holds.
Permit me to say a word about shipping space. In
connection with the commodity price question, I assure
you that Japan at present pays the highest ocean freights.
In fact, Japan is the only country in the world paying
such high rates.
Japanese Shipping
The reason is obvious. Despite the enormous number
of bottoms needed by the war, we are not permitted to
charter foreign vessels, which is why ocean traffic is
congested and congested traffic boosts freights. Japan's
shipping is now trying to carry two tons where capacity
for only one ton exists.
Supposing we were to charter from 300,000 to
500,0o0 tons of foreign vessels, the ocean freights
would at once come down, which would also lower the
commodity prices. of course, prevention of the outflow
of gold is the reason for not allowing the import of
ships and foreign charters. Can higher freight and
commodity prioes be tolerated, as long as we keep
imported vessels our shores? Or must we permit impor-
tation of ships in order to lower freight and commodity
prioes? Those are questions worth serious pondering
over, I presume. The government shipped out ¥300-
million specie last year. Why doesn't it release more
gold specie?
Tsuda: I am going to speak about gold strictly
as a layman. As far as I know, there are lying in
Japan's spheres of influence enormous quantities of
gold, partioularly in Manchoukuo. Despite the pessimism
voioed

=== Page 328 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
voiced by some prospectors, there is more gold there
than is generally realized. If the government shows
an interest and facilitates the mining of gold, the
amount produced will, I assure you, be surprising.
Why don't you release all the gold the government
holds, trusting that the metal to be mined will take
ample care of the country's needs.
Ishiwata: Messrs. Wurata and Tsuda believe that
there is a sufficient amount of gold available. All
depends on what assurance we can get as to future
security. As a protracted war requires considerable
money, I cannot very well release the gold specie.
However, after considering the questions of foreign
trade, greater productive capacity issue, commodity
prices, etc. we may release some if we come to the
conclusion that suoh is the best way. It should be
remembered that we are carrying on a war and that we
must accept the ohallenge of protracted resistance.
Murata: Germany has been running the country on
a surprisingly small amount of money ...
Ishiwata: Foreign trade and restricted commodity
consumption are what have enabled Germany to do what
she has. I think if Japan made up her mind to follow
the German plan, on the assumption that she did not
have ¥5o0-million in gold, she could do much while
retaining the gold. But everything will become more
acute and serious, I believe.

1939 COMPULSORY GOLD CENSUS
(From THE JAPAN TIMES & MAIL, May 21, 1939.)
All Gold Articles To Be Registered
With Proper Authorities By July 5
Only Possessions of Absolute Need
Exempted from Government Decree
All privately owned gold articles, excepting gold
teeth, penpoints and other items of absolute necessity
or from which ertraction of the metal is difficult,
must be registered with the Government from July 1 to
5, it was gazetted tod ay in accordance with the 12th
Article of the Gold Production Law.
Each household and organization will receive a
card from the authorities in which to enter all gold
articles in possessed by them. The items to be reported
cover all personal ornaments, household utensils and
stationery containing the precious metal. Exceptions
are gold teeth, needles for medical use, nozzles for
rayon production, lightning-conductors, fountain pens,
badges and initial marks.
Articles to be registered are divided into the
following five categories:
1. Personal ornaments including rings,
watch-chains, medals, bracelets, sash-clips,
hair ornaments, buttons, necktie-pins, brooches
and others.
2. Eye-glass frames, watch-frames, compacts,
rouge-containers, gold parts of handbags, canes,
and other personal effects.
3. Smoking articles including pipes, cigar-
end cutters and lighters.
4. Cups, kettles and other tableware includ-
ing spoons, plates, sake-bottles and tea things.
5. Candle-stands, incense-burners and other
household utensils including trophies, photograph-
stands, weights for kakemono, etc.
6. Stationery including pen and paper knives,
seals, ink-pad oases, paper-weights, etc.
Those who fail to report or make false entries will
be liable to fines not exoeeding ¥500.

ENCOURAGEMENT TO ALLUVIAL GOLD MINING
(From THE OSAKA MAINICHI, March 26, 1939)
ALLUVIAL GOLD MINE PROSPECTING SUBJECT
TO SUBSIDY SYSTEM
Program Extended
Promotion Company Act Now Revised;
Refineries To Be Increased
The Japan Gold Mining Industry Promotion Co. Act
and the gold mining encouragement regulations have
been revised so that subsidies may be given for
prospecting alluvial gold deposits. As a result of
the revision of the act and regulations, the Commerce
Office included 3,000,000 gold mine prospecting sub-
sidies and 4,690,000 gold refinery construction
subsidies (within one half of the funds required for
the construction) in the office's budget for the
1939-40 fiscal year.
The Japan Gold Mining Industry Promotion Co. has
been established by the Commerce Office in order to
increase the gold production in Japan. The gold pro-
duction in Japan has been encouraged by not only the
company, but also the government by giving direct
subsidies for prospecting gold mines.
Losses To Be Indemnified
In order to promote the exploitation of new gold
mines and to increase the gold refining capacity in
Japan, the Japan Gold Mining Industry Promotion Co.
Aot has been revised. As a result of the revision,
the government will indemnify the losses, whioh may be
caused by the promotion company's making advances and
giving subsidies for gold mine prospecting and refinery
construction by order of the government.
At the same time, the Commerce Office's direct
subsidy system has been ertended to prospecting of
alluvial gold deposits. In order to make the applica-
tion procedure easier, the gold mining encouragement
regulations also have been revised, effective April 1.
The Japan Gold Mining Industry Promotion Co. is to
make advanoes for prospeoting gold mines for the period
of four years (commenoing from the l939-40 fiscal year
up to the 1942-43 fisoal year) at the rate of about
100,000 in Japan proper and ¥100,000 in Chosen. From
five to l0 mines are qualified to receive the advances.
Regarding

=== Page 333 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8 -
Regarding the gold refinery, the erstwhile polioy
was to refine gold ore including pure gold of from
6/1,000,000 to 7/1,000,000 because the refining of such
ore can be conducted on the commercial basis. During
the 1939-40 fiscal year, however, 10 refineries, which
are capable of refining ore including pure gold of from
2/1,000,000 to 5/1,000,000, wi11 be established.
About ¥2,500,000 will be required for the con-
struction of such refineries. The Japan Gold Mining
Industry Promotion Co. will finance them, while the
government will indemnify the losses of the company
to the total amount and a part of the operation funds
will be shouldered by the government.
(FrOm THE JAPAN ADVERTISER of May 21, 1939)
Also in line with the Jovernment's campaign to obtain
more gold, the Japan Gold ining Development Corporation,
at a meeting of its board of directors on Priday, decided
to construct gold smelters at places where low-grade ore
is be ing mined. Out of a total of 10 such smelters to
be built within five years throughout the country, three
will be built this year, one each in the northeastern
part of the main island, in the Hokkaido and in Kyushu.
The refinery in the northeast will be put up at the
village of Higoroichi, iwate Prefecture, at a cost of
Yen 800,o00 with a capac1ty of 10u tons of ore a day and
employing more than l00 people. Assaying, refining and
handling equipment is scheduled to be comple ted by the
end of this year.

(THE JAPAN TIMES & MAIL, March 10, 1939.)
FUND READY TO AID GOLD
Government Wil1 Disburse ¥28,000,000 to
Encourage Production, Official Says
The Government will disburse approximately
¥28,000,000 to encourage production of gold during
the fisoal year 1939-40, the financial bureau chief
of the Finance Ministry said in replying to interpel-
lation made by Kiyone Kawamata, Social Mass Party, and

¥28,O00,O00 under both Special and General Accounts.
of this total, about 10,190,000 is to be transferred
from the Special Account of the Gold Fund, while
¥4,340,000 is to be expended by the Commerce and Industry
Ministry. These disbursements shall be made to give aid
to prospecting, training of technicians and other gold
production encouragement undertakings.
The proposed bill is to authorize the Government
to indemnify losses which may be incurred by the Nippon
Gold Production Promotion Company in connection with its
enterprises conducted in accordance with orders issued
by the Government for the encouragement of gold produc-
tion, and also provides that such indemnification by the
Government shali be made within an amount approved by
the Imperial Diet.

(From Mitsubishi Economic Research ureaa  IRctLAR

REVISION OF GOLD FUND SPECIAL ACC UNT LAW
The dold Fund Special Account was establishec on
August 25, 1937, with funds amounting to 747 million yen
collected from the Bank of Japan, the Bank of Chosen and
the Bank of Taiwan according to the Jold Reserve Revalua-
tion Law, which was enforced at the same time.
The old Law authorized the Government to earmark part
of the fund held by this account u. to 50 million yen for
the encouragement of gold minin, to purchase newly mined
gold and to invest in Goverrnment bonds.
According to the revised Law, which was enforced on
March 24, 1939, funds of this special account will also be
available for the acquisition of debentures and shares to
be issued by the Japan Gold .ining Development Com any as
well as for other investments desi nated by Iaperial
Ordinance.
AMENDMENT TO THE GOLD PRODUCTION AW
Since August, 1937, when the Gold Production Law was
put into force, the Government have made efforts to promote
the output of gold on the one and, and to save and collect
gold by regulations relating to restrictions on the use of
gold. In view of the increased necessity of replenishing
the gold reserve for international payments, the Law has
been revised so as to authorize the Government to effect
the compulsory purchase of gold. According to the revision,
the Government may prohibit or restrict the disposal of gold
specie or bullion, designated gold alloys or designated
articles which are made mainly of gold, and may order the
sale of these articles to the Government or other parties
designated by the Government.

FIDUCIARY NOTE ISSUE EXPANSION.
(Summaries from Mitsubishi Economic Research Bureau
MONTHLY CIRCULAR No. 187 of May, 1939, pages 3 and 11.)
The maximum limit of the fiduciary issue of bank
notes of the Bank of Japan, the Bank of Chosen and the
Bank of Taiwan will be expanded by 500 million, 60
million and 30 million yen respectively as a result of
a Law approved by the 74tr session of tne Imperial Diet.
Temporary Expansion of the Limit of Fiduciary
Note Issue of Bank of Japan
(Law No. 58 of March 31, 1939)
The limit of fiduciary note issue of the Bank of
Japan was expanded from 120 million yen to 1,o00 million
yen in July, 1927, wiici. limit has been maintained until
last year. In view of the marked increase in the demand
for funds and the pronounced pro ress of cconomic
activity during the past decade and particularly after
the outbreak of hostilities, the linit was raised to
1,700 million yen last year by Law No. 4 of March 31,
1938. During the previous year, however, the actual
note issue of the Bank of Janan showeu a sharp increase
with the resultant appearance of an excess issue over
the legal limit. This excess issue has been caused
partly by the remarkable increase of business trans-
actions and partly by the removal of 300 million yen of
gold from the reserve of the Bank of Japan to the Gold
Fund Special Account in July last year. As a further
increase of the note issue is considered unavoidable.
the limit has again been raised to 2, 200 million yen
by the new Law. As a result of this revision, notes
of the Bank of Japan may be issued to the limit of
2,70l million yen without taxation, including notes
issued on the basis of the gold reserve amounting to
501 million yen at present. The new Law was enforced
fron April lst.
Temporary Expansion of the Limit of Fiduciary Note Issue
of the Bank of Chosen and the Bank of Taiwan
The limit of fiduciary note issue of the Bank of
Chosen and Bank of Taiwan, which was raised in September,
1937, from 50 million yen to 100 million yen and from 20
million yen to 50 million yen respectively, was again
expanded to 160 million yen and 80 million yen respec-
tively by Law No. 59 of March 31, 1939. The revision will
be enforced from May lst. As a result of this expansion,
the notes of the Bank of Japan used as a reserve for the
notes of the colonial banks will be freed to the extent
of the expansion, an aggregate amount of 90 million yen.

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.99)
(From THE ORIENTAL ECONOMIST of May, 1939, page 330.)
BANK OF JAPAN NOTES
The Bank of Japan was established in 1882 as
central bank under the Statute of the Bank of Japan
of l882. It is a joint-stock bank in organization
with head-office in Tokyo, but its Governor and Deputy
Governor are appointed by the Government. It has an
exclusive right of issuing bank notes for the whole
of Japan except Taiwan and Chosen where the Bank of
Taiwan and the Bank of Chosen respectively have similar
privileges as banks of issue. The Statute of Convertible
Bank Notes Issued by the Bank of Japan provides:
1. That the Bank of Japan shall carry in
reserve against its notes issued gold and silver
coins and bullion of equivalent value and the
value of silver coins and bullion must not exceed
one-fourth of the total reserve:
2. That the Bank of Japan may issue, in
addition to the amounts specified in the preceding
paragraph, bank-notes to the amount of 1,000,000,000
against Government bonds, Treasury certificates and
other "giltedged" securities or commercial bills:
3. That the Bank of Japan, in case of neces-
sity, may issue bank-notes in excess of such
¥1,000,000,000, against securities as prescribed
in the foregoing paragraph. In case such excess
issue is to continue for more than 15 days, the
sanction of the Finance Minister is required, and
on a tax not less than 3% per annum as prescribed
by the Finance Minister shall be levied.
By virtue of Law No. 64 of April 1, 1938, the
1,O00,000,000 fiduciary issue privilege specified in
Items 2 and 3 had been raised to ¥1,700,000,000 and this
amount was further raised to ¥2,200,000,000 in April 1,
1939, effective until further notice.
As a matter of fact, the Bank of Japan's specie
reserve contains no silver coins or bullion. Since
December 17, 1931, Japan has been on a non-redemption
basis.

INTERATICRAL PAYIMNTS

=== Page 341 === (confidence 0.99)
(From THE ORIENTAL ECONOMIST, May 1939, pages 294 and 295.)
INTERNATIONAL PAYMNTS SINCE 1937
In 1937 the Finance Department abandoned its
practice of making public its survey of invisible trade
results, and this has given rise to the impression that
the nation's invisible trade position must have deterio-
rated to a great extent. Some foreigners have published
their own figures based upon conjecture, but these can-
not of course be relied upon. Until the Finance Depart-
ment changes its mind and resumes publication of its
figures, the true status of Japan's international payments
must remain a sealed book. But it is hardly imaginable
that conditions in the more recent years could be improved
when compared with those prior to 1937, as is sugested
by the fact that Japan is continuing to make gold ship-
ments. While an accurate calculation is impossible, as
studied from various angles and exclusive of that with
Manchoukuo, the invisible trade in 1937 is believeu to
have given Japan a favorable balance althou  very small.
As in that year Japan made capital oxports to i.anchoukuo
in excess of ¥500 million, even after deducting inward
payments Japan's invisible trade with that country
probably resulted in a 2o0 million unfavorable balance.
Manchoukuo today, however, in the economic sense at least
cannot be correctly classed as a forein country, and such
a balance as the above cannot rightly be included in
Japan's items of international payments. The real balance
of 1937 international payments for Japan therefore must
be the difference between a certain export surplus from
the above mentioned invisible trade with foreign countries
excepting Manchoukuo, and an import surplus from visible
trade with foreign countries excepting Manchoukuo. As
has been announced by the Governnent, this real balance
is believed to have been of such a size that it was just
about offset by that year's old shipments of 860 million.
Invisible trade conditions last year, excepting with
Manchoukuo, were unexpectedly good, and the trade very
nearly balanced. Consequently the 1938 balance of Japan's
international payments about corresponded to the balance
of visible trade in that year. The year's merchendise
trade of the Japanese empire resulted in an export surplus
of ¥60 million. This figure comprises not only trade with
Manchoukuo, but also Japan's trade with China, quite a
percentage of which last year cannot be rightly classified
as foreign trade movement. To arrive at any true balance
of international payments these factors will have to be
taken into consideration although the process is neither
simple nor easy. In particular segregating the volume
of non-foreign trade movement from the China trade figure
may prove wellnigh impossible. However, judging from gold
and silver movements as well as foreign exoharge turnover,
Japan's

=== Page 342 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Japan's balance of international payments last year
is believed to have been in the red by 500 million
more or less.
It is still too early to form any idea about the
probable course of Japan's international payments for
the current year. Even excluding those destined for
Manchoukuo anu China, there is every prospect that
exports to world narkets will show some improverent
over the previous year. Higher raw silk values are
one of the added factors pointing in that direction.
By the functioning of the supply mobilization plan,
imports will be regulated so as to balance with exports
and no radical excess inflow of merchendise ean occur.
While it is a foregone conclusion that Japen wili have
another debit balance of world payments this year, it
is also patent that the balance cannot become very
large and will be restricted to a point where it can
be amply met by gold and silver shipments and the sale
abroad of Japanese owned forein securities.
On May 1 redemption was made ir London of I1,787,600
City of Osaka bonds maturing on that day. his was an
extra burden that was not carried last yeur, but us the
amount involved was smali ana as a portion of the bonds
had already passed into apanese possession, the redemp-
tion operations involved no particular di Piculty. Vith
this repayment now out of the way, no important bond
maturity abroad is in sight until 1943.

=== Page 343 === (confidence 0.99)
(Editorial in THE OSAKA MAINICHI, March 22, 1939.)
EXPANSION OF EXPORT LOAN SYSTEM
By improving the export loan system, or the system
of indemnity for the loss by the exchange banks from the
advance of loans to exporters, the Commerce Office has
expanded its scope of application. With a view to
developing the export trade, the system was inaugurated
last August with a ¥4,700,000 appropriation therefor.
Since then, it has served its purpose with considerable
success. The number of export loans made by the banks
by the end of February has reached 2,433 cases and
¥14,880,000 in term of money.
Out of these cases the goverrent was only called
upon once actually to pay in indeanification, the amount
being about ¥5o0. Thus the system has accomplished a
far better result than had been anticipated. In view
of this favorable showing, it is quite an appropriate
action on the part of the Commerce authorities that they
have enlarged the scope of its application at this time
further to expedite our export trade.
In order to realize the objective of the hostilities
through an abundant supply of munitions and other neces-
sary materials for augmenting the productive capacity
from outside under the long-term construction campaign,
it is hardly necessary to mention that the nation must
develop its purchasing power abroad by providing all the
necessary measures for the development of the export
trade. The government, being fully aware of the vital
importance of bolstering exports, has instituted various
expediencies since last year such as, (1) establishing
a special link system on each type of commodity, thus
helping to mitigate the difficulty of importing raw
materials to some extent, (2) establishing an account
for the foreign exchange fund by setting aside a part
of the Bank of Japan's specie reserve, and (3) pro-
viding measures by which surplus funds of exchange
banks can be acoumulated by the Bank of Japan. Thus,
from the angles of commodities, finance, and exchange,
the authorities have done much to vitalize the export
trade development facilities and thereby have carried
out a phenomenal resuscitation of the foreign trade
polioy.
Especially in order to develop the export of sundry
goods in general and others, the nation depends much
upon the work of the industrialists and businessmen of
small and medium means who have more or less infirm
oredit and feeble capital. It follows then that it is
most urgent as an export development measure to give
very

=== Page 344 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
very smooth financial access to these classes of people.
For that purpose, the export indemnity law was
promulgated in 1930. It has had a favorable effect
on our trade, but as it provides an indemnity only to
the extent of 80 per cent after the goods are shipped
out and the transaction completed, the medium and small
businessmen and industrialists have found the law far
short of their neeus as what they require is a fund
for buying raw materials for the manufacture of export
goods. The current export loan system has been brought
about to cover the shortcomins of the export indemnity
law. This system permits the exchange banks to advance
loans within the amount of the exnort oods ordered
from middle and small businessmen on their personal
credit. In the event ban's suffer losses from such
an arrangement as the result of the inauility of these
exporters to repay the banks the money they borrow,
the government indemnifies the banks up to 80 per cent
of the loss. It must indecd be deemed quite an appro-
priate exoort trade measure.
After studying the actual results of the export
loan system in the past, various revisions of the
regulations have been made to remedy the defects.
While tnose who are qualified to obtain advances had
been limited to members of an exporters' association
or other bodies recognized by the Commerce Office,
the qualifications have been extended to the exporters'
association itself (not only members), members of the
association, and traders who have been engaged in
export business for more than two years consecutively
and who hold a bank's confidenec.
In obtaining an advance from an export bank, the
exporter had to submit to the bank a certificate of
the association to whicn the exporter belongs, proving
that he had received a buying order fron abroad. Such
a certificate will no longer be required, but the loan
will be made subject to approval of the contract by a
bank.
According to the former system, an exporter was
obliged to refund the loan to the amount equal to the
indemnified sum, except when the exporter was unable
to refund the loan even up to the indemnified extent.
This has been revised so that the government will
shoulder such losses of the bank, when they are caused
by force majeure and the government considers it in-
appropriate to let the borrower refund the loan en-
tirely or partly. This is quite favorable for exporters
and will be very effective in promoting Japan's export
business.
The export loan system, however, is limited to
exporters. Producers of export goods are not favored
by such a system. Since the speciality of Japan's
export business lies in the fact that medium and minor
manufacturers, from the financial viewpoint, constitute
the

=== Page 345 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
the mainstay of the organization, the financial support
of these manufacturers is necessary in order to promote
Japan's export business.
If these manufacturers are unable to meet the
exporters' buying orders due to slow production,
following the restricted supply of funds, the promotion
of Japan's export, which is now considerea important
and imminent. will be impossihle.
The government, therefore, should extend the export
loan system immediately and the export goods production
loan indemnification system should also be establisned
in order to mitigate the shortage of fund supply to the
export goods manufacturers. It is desirable that the
export loan system in. parallel with the export goods
production indemnification system wili runction smoothly
toward the promotion of Japan's export business.

MEMORANDUM
Amerioan Consulate, Yokohama, Japan, August 18, 1939.
To:
Mr. Boyee.
From:
Mr. White.
Subjeot: Importation of "Cheap" Yen into Yokohama.
The promulgation of Departmental Ordinances No. 27
and No. z8 of the Ministry of Finance, under date of
June 23, 1939, prohibited the 1mportation without permit
of more than Yen 2o0 of Japanese bank notes.
This ordinance has arrested, to a degree, a trend
whioh, through the past thirteen months, has substantial-
ly reduced the inward or credit movements in Japan's
balance of international payments.
Inoome oategories in Yokohama whioh have, to the
knowledge of the Consulate, been reduced through the
importation and use in Japan of "cheap" yen, in lieu of
foreign currencies, include:
1. Expenditures by officers and seamen of foreign
vessels.
2. Expenditures by returning emigrants.
3. Tourist expenditures.
4.
Exponditures by foreign religious and eleemos-
ynary institutions.
5.
Expenditures by resident foreigners in Japan
having sources of income abroad.
1. Expenditures by ofricers and seamen of foreign vessels.
During 1938, 1,220 foreign vessels called at the
port of Yokohama. Entrances during 1939 have been in
approximately the same ratio. Prior to the drop in the
exchange value abroad of the yen, crew members of foreign
vessels brought into Yokohama quantities of foreign ex-
change which were converted looally into yen.
Since the depreciation of the yen abroad, this pro-
cedure has come to an almost complete stop. Crew members
of ships stopping at Chinese ports have converted their
curreney in China. Crew members on other vessels have
either purohased yen at favorable rates in Honolulu or
San Franciseo or have purchased them in one of Yokohama's
bootleg exchange centers.
One of these centers was reported to be a bar owned
by an American. This Ameriean and one other are currently
under arrest for exchange violations. Their procedure
was

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
was alleged to include the purohase of dollars and ster-
ling 1n Yokohama at a discount (Y.4.00-5.50 to $1.00),
and the conveyanoe of the foreign currenoy to Shanghai
where yen were repurchased at rates which varied from Y.
8.00 to 11.00 per $1.00.
2. Expenditures by returning emigrants.
During 1938 quantities of Japanese bank notes arrived
in Honolulu, California ports, and New York. These were
brought into American ports in some cases by seamen who
had purchased them at low rates in 3hanghai. In one
other case, bank notes were reportedly smuggled out of
Japan into the United States by a foreign minin company.
This currenoy has reportedly been purchased at a
discount by Japanese resident in Americe who were return-
ing to Japan for a visit or to retire.
3. Tourist expenditures.
Tourists arriving in Japan via Chinese ports have
during recent months made a practice of purchasing sufficient
yen to meet their needs in Japan.
Tourists embar ing in the nited tates can purchase
yen at a disoount before leaving or from crew members
who have made exchange transactions a secondery occupa-
tion. Such transactlons have been quite openly concluded
on ships of both the Empress and President Lines. These
practices have been the occasion of bitter complaints
by Yokohama licensed exchange brokers.
4. Bxponditures by foreign religious and eleemosynary
inatitutiong.
Expenditures in Japan by foreign religious and chari-
table organizations have constituted in the past a source
of invisible income. During the past year a number of
these organizations have had all or part of their foreign
contributions sent direotly to Shanghai for conversion
into yen. The yen have then been brought into Japan by
members of the different organizations. In one case,
the organization has sent a staff member to Shanghai each
month for this specifio purpose. In another case, an
organization representative upon arrival in Japan declared
her curreney, amounting to more than Y.10,000, but was
permitted to import it because it was for a legitimate
purpose.
5. Expenditurea by resident foreigners in Japan having
souroee of Ineome abroed.
Two foreign consular officials in Yokohame have made
trips to China for the avowed purpose of converting their
foreign

=== Page 351 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
foreign salaries into Japan bank notes. There also reside
in Yokohama a number of foreigners who have retired and
who have pensions or other sources of income from abroad.
In addition to the Shanghai market, these persons have
been able to convert their funds from abroad by purchases
from persons in Japan who have "blocked" yen.
Purchases in Yokohama of Foreign Currency.
During the period, June 1938 - April 1939, local
residents took advantage of the provision i: the Foreign
Exchange Law which allowed, without permit, the purchase
locally for traveling expenses, of Yen 5cc in foreign
currency drafts or traveler's checks, and the same amount
in foreign currenoy.
This provision permitted residents, because of the
difference in exchange rates, to truvel to China, make
purohases, and return to Japan, without expense. In April
1939, a curb was placed on this trend by the Finance
Ministry's ruling that the purchase of foreign curren-
cies in Japan was not valid for travel to the "yen
bloe" areas. Residents the resorted to the practice of
purohasing transportation to Manila; the "cheap" yen
being purchased in Shanghai on the return voyage.
This same procedure was placed on a commercial basis
by some foreigners who employed local residents to
obtain foreign currencies under false declarations of
intent to travel abroad. The authorities curbed this
practioe by instruoting the banks to sell drafts and
ourrenoies only when the applicant could prove that he
was a bona fide traveler.
Undervaluation of Exports.
There is some evidenee that local exporters have
taken advantage of the difference in exchenge rates. One
case which has come to the attention of the Consulate,
involved an Indian exporter who operated in the following
manner. The exporter's affiliate, who operated a branch
in India, would order Japanese merchandise worth Yen 50,000,
but stated as worth only Yen 15,000 in the order. The
local exporter would draw on the foreign agent for foreign
ourrenoy equivalent to Yen 15,000 and would obtain the
customs' export permit for the same amount. The shipment
of goods would aotually be worth Yen 50,O00. The looal
exporter would then send an agent to Shanghai to receive
1n foreign ourreney the balance of Yen 35,000. Part of
the foreign ourreney would be used to purohase "cheap"
yen and the balanoe banked abroad as profit.
This exporter and several other Indian exporters
hurriedly

=== Page 352 === (confidence 0.99)
hurriedly left Japan when it became apparent that the
police were investigating their activities.
Comments
The larger part of this curreney movement was, prior
to July 1, 1939, entirely legal under Japanese law. It
represented a diversion of foreign funds which would
otherwise have beer spent in Japan.
The only three procedures which, to the knowled e
of the Consulate, have been held to be illegal, were:
1. The purchase and sale of ioreign currencies within
Japan.
2.
The purchase through "dummies" of foreign currenoy
drafts and traveler's checks.
3. Undervaluation of exports.
There is no way of estimating the foreign exchange
loss sustained by Japan through the importation of yen,
in liou of foreign currencies. From evidence available
in Yokohama it appears that the greatest percentage reduc-
tions have taken place in crew and tourist expenditures
of foreign funds. In this connection, it is of interest
to note that an interpreter attached to the foreign ex-
change investigation unit has informed a consular officer
that imports into Japan of "cheap" yen by foreigners have
been on a small soale as corpared with imports by Japanese
subjeots. He said that there hud been many arrests of
Japanese for exchange violations but that, contrary to
the practice "ith foreigners, the arrests had been given
no publicity.
The new regulation will presumably cause a reduction
in the volume of yen importation. Many persons, who
proviously brought in yen because it was legal as well
as profitable, will presumably limit their imports to the
Yen zoo allowed without permit. On the other hand, the
recent continued depreciation of the yen in Shanghai will
make smuggling more profitable than it was in 1938 and the
first part of 1939. The Consulate has been reliably
informed of two cases of yen imports whioh have been made
since July 1. In one case 10,000 yen had been purchased
for 1,Ooo American dollars. The current practioe of
customs ofricials in Yokohama is not to seareh any person
disembarking unless they have reason to believe that he
is importing currenoy.
Since July 1, the Consulate has had its attention
called to three cases of Amerieans who have taken advan-
tage of what is apparently a loophole in the eurrent regula-
tions. These persons have gone to Japanese banks in
Shanghai

=== Page 353 === (confidence 0.96)
- 5 -
Shanghai and Peking and have, by presenting yen, been
permitted to buy bank drafts on the Yokohama Specie Bank,
payable in Japan. In one case, no questions were asked.
In the other two cases, it was necessary for the applicants
to eetablish to the satiefaction of the bank ofricials
that the funds requested were for the individuals own
use and not for speculative ourposes. Their yen were
bought at an average of 0.08 cents American, in contrast
to 27.5 cents Amerlcan if they had been purchased in
Japan.
van Bawhite

=== Page 355 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
November 22, 1939
Referenoe a report from the American Con-
sul at Kobe entitled "Japan's Gold Situation",
September 30, 1939.
Since gold shipments were resumed by the
Japanese Government in March 1937, total ship-
ments of gold from Japan to the United States
have amounted to l.9 billion yen.
During the first three quarters of 1939
Japan exported to the United States gold valued
at more than 366.3 million yen, this being an
average of 40.7 mill1on yen per month.
Gold production in Japan is apparently
not proceeding favorably. Production has been
described as "rather poor" and at a "low
level". It appears that production goals will
not be achieved. with the sale to the Japanese
Government of the gold mining properties of the
Oriental Consolidated Mining Company, all
foreign gold mining concessions in the Japanese
Empire have passed into Japanese hands. The
company was sold for Us$8,175,000, and accord-
ing to the agreement these funds are to be
remitted to the United States over a period
extending to August 31, 1943.
Produotion of gold within Japan appears

Total Shipments in Last Three Years
Since gold shipments were resumed in March 1937,
the total shipments of gold from Japan to the United
1
States have totaled 483.74 metric tons valued at 1.9
2
billion yen.
1939 Gold Exports from Japan
During the first three quarters of 1939 Japan
3
exported to the United States 88.4 metric tons of
gold valued at more than 366.3 million yen.
The monthly average of gold shipments has there-
fore been about 9.8 metric tons valued at 40.7 million
yen. Shipments in June and again in August and
September amounted to more than 1l metric tons a month.
No Admitted Reduction in Bank Gold Reserves in 1939
In order to ship out over 22ot metric tons of gold
in 1937 and 175 metric tons in 1938 it has been admitted
by the Japanese Government that bank gold reserves were
drawn upon. In 1939, however, the gold reserves of the
Bank of Japan are publicly recorded as remaining intact
5
at ¥50l,287,000. In quantity the reserves would perhaps
6
amount to 145.38 metric tons.

7
Need of Increased Gold Production
Gold production in the Japanese Empire is undoubted-
ly greater than the annual output of 41 metric tons in
1936, the latest year for which official statistics are
available. In view of public admissions that the goals
of 51 metric tons in 1937 and 64 metric tons in 1938
appear not to have been attained, there is, however,
much reason to believe that not enough old is newly
mined to cover monthly exports averaging 9.8 metric tons
in 1939, much less industrial and other needs in addition,
even with restrictions on the use of gold.
The present policy of secrecy hides the true gold
production situation, but statements that get out in
spite of the censors show that not only is there a
strongly felt need for more gold production but also
that the output of newly mined gold is still disappoint-
ing.
Factors Adversely Affecting Production
Gold production in the Japanese Empire during the
first six months of 1939 has been described as "rather
poor" and at a "low level". It has also been said that
"the situation in the field does not make it possible
to realize the production goal" and that "results are
unsatisfactory under the prevailing conditions."
One of the adverse factors has been the present
shortage of labor. To remedy this recommendations have
been
7. More details are given in Attachment 2 to this report.

=== Page 362 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
been made not only that more Chosenese (Korean) and
Chinese workers should be employed in the gold mines
but also that juvenile labor should be sanctioned in
the pits. Japanese Government departments have also
been urged to help provide laborers for gold mining as
an industry of vital concern to Japan.
Stress has been laid on the damage done by heavy
snowfalls last winter with probable reference especially
to Chosen (Korea) and possibly to "Manchukuo". Most of
the medium-sized and small companies are financially
8
hard up. The Gold Production Encouragement Company,
half-owned by the Japanese Government, in spite of
Japan's recognized need for gold, is not inclined to
make loans to firms of this type without sufficient
collateral.
Suggested Active Participation of Government in
Gold Mining
The suggestion has been made that gold production
should be undertaken directly by the Japanese Government.
This would prevent mines from remaining idle and would
permit the development of mining properties that are
not sufficiently worked because the owners lack funds.
This would mean active participation and would go beyond
the subsidies and other help already being given by the
9
Government.

from the Kobe Consulate on "Japan's Gold Problem".

Even if the Government should act on such a sug-

by the powerful Nissan, Sumitomo, Mitsui, Mitsubishi,

Disappearance of Foreign Gold Mining Companies
All foreign gold mining concessions in the Japanese
Empire are now understood to have passed into Japanese
hands. Towards the end of the nineteenth century a
number of gold mining properties were acquired by
foreigners. By the end of 1937, the year of the out-
break of the "China incident", all had been sold to the
Japanese with the exception of the Oriental Consolidated
Mining Company's extensive and rich Unsan property in
northwestern Chosen (Korea). After the beginning of
the trouble in China, with Japanese Government exchange
restrictions which made it more and more difficult to
get money out of the country for dividends and purchases
of materials and equipment, the American owmers of this
property saw the writing on the wall and had no hopes
of renewing the concession upon the expiration of their
rights in 1954. The only thing which seemed left to
do was to keep on working the concession and in the
meantime

Hopes of Getting Gold in New Ways
In its strictly confidential letter authorizing
the Nippon Mining Company to send money out of the
country in payment for the American gold concession,
the Finance Ministry, as it has many times before, laid
14
great emphasis on the need of increasing production.
In addition to getting more gold out of mines of
the usual type, there is interest in obtaining gold
from other sources and in new ways.
There is, for example, on foot a scheme to get
alluvial gold by a new method of draining and dredging
the seabed off a part of the coast of Chosen.
One inventor has also been endeavoring to get
support for a pump and hose gold sifter which he claims
can profitably work poor alluvial deposits with less
than eight parts of gold to a million parts of sand.

New Mint Facilities
In August it was announced that the new Tokyo mint
will use two electric and 3 gas furnaces.
16
No Change in Official Gold Price
The Finance Ministry is still not inclined to raise
the official purchasing price of gold which has remained
at ¥3.85 a gram since May 2, 1938, because of the Govern-
ment's low price policy, although in some quarters it is
believed that there is danger of increased gold smuggling
if the price is not adjusted.

NEED OF INCREASED GOLD PRODUCTION
(From THE JAPAN ADVERTISER, August 19, 1939)
GOLD MINERS URGED TO PRODUCE LIORE
Bureau of Mines Issues Instructions to
Producers to Concentrate on Output
Because of the unsatisfactory returns in the pro-
duction of gold for the first half of this year, the
bureau of mines of the Commerce and Industry Ministry
has issued instructions to all gold mining companies
to concentrate more intensely on their production,
according to the Yomiuri.
The semi-annual production reports which the all
Japanese mining concerns are required to file with the
bureau indicate that results for the first six months
of this year have been rather poor says the journal.
Members of the Gold Production Increase Council,
which is attached to the Ministry, are said to have
explained that the low level of production was the
result of heavy snowfalls last winter and the prevail-
ing shortage of labor. Nevertheless, the bureau is
said to have decided to urge producers to make good
their first six months' production during the second
half of this year in view of the importance of gold
in meeting Japan's international payments during the
emergency.
Mr. Yoshida, managing-director of the Japan Ore
Refining Company, is quoted by the Miyako as having
made the following statement:
"It is true that an increase in the production
of gold is urgent and important to meet the present
wartime situation. However, the situation in the field
does not make it possible to realize the production
goal. It is natural that results are unsatisfactory
under the prevailing conditions. Most of the medium-
sized and small companies are financially hard up. The
Gold Production Encouragement Company, a national policy
institution, is supposed to lend money to production
companies, but it will not make loans without sufficient
collateral. In my opinion, the gold production industry
should be undertaken directly by the Government."
(From

=== Page 377 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
(From THE JAPAN TIMES, August 19, 1939)
STRICT ORDERS ISSUED TO UP GOLD OUTPUT
Strict orders were issued by the Commerce and
Industry Ministry Thursday against certain important
gold mine operators, requiring the latter to increase
their gold production during the latter half of the
current year.
Under the provision of Article 7 of the Gold
Production Law the Commerce and Industry Ministry
authorities caused all the cold mine operators to
submit their gold production increase plans for the
first half of the present year, with a view to attain-
ing substantial increase in th production of the
precious metal at each mine.
It was found recently, however, that the actual
gold production record of certain important mines for
the first half year was not up to the plans reported
by their operators. Causes of the inactiveness of the
gold mines in question are said to be found in the
snow damage of last Winter and a shortage in supply
of miners.
However, the Commerce and Industry Ministry issued
strict orders requiring the operators to increase their
production during the latter half irrespective of the
circumstances in order to make up for the poor produc-
tion record.
(From THE JAPAN CHRONICLE, August 4, 1939)
TO INCREASE GOLD PRODUCTION
Round-Table Conference Called by Commerce Minister
Four Points Discussed
A round-table conference of Government officials
and civilians concerned with the gold mining industry
was called by the Commerce Minister at the Industrial
Club in Tokyo on Wednesday. Mr. Hatta, the Commerce
Minister, has been endeavouring, ever since he assumed
office, to encourage the increased production of gold
from the standpoint of improving the country's inter-
national accounts.
Mr. Imai, Parliamentary Under-Secretary of the
Commerce Department, Mr. Murase, the Vice-Minister,
Mr. Sawada, Counsellor, Mr. Kogane, chief of the
Mining

=== Page 378 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
Mining Bureau, and Mr. Hosokawa, chief of the Gold
Production Section, were present at the conference
which was attended by a large number of businessmen,
experts and others.
Views were freely exchanged and the following
items were then discussed: a) smooth supply of labourers;
b) resumption of operations in idle mines; c) establish-
ment of new gold refineries; and d) reduction of freight
rates for ores.
(From THE JAPAN CHRUNICLE, August 10, 1939)
TO INCREASE GOLD OUTPUT
Third Round-Table Conference Discusses Plans
Causes of Poor Results
The third round-table conference to discuss the
increased gold production plan was held at the Patents
Bureau on Tuesday afternoon under the chairmanship of
Mr. Kogane, chief of the Mining Bureau of the Commerce
Department.
Many Government officials including the section
chiefs concerned and representatives of the Bank of
Japan, the Japan Gold Production Company and eleven
gold producers were present, and views were freely
exchanged regarding measures to increase gold produc-
tion based on the output for the first half of this year.
It was pointed out that during the first half of
this year, 27 gold mines returned particularly poor
production figures. As for the reasons for the poor
mining results, detailed explanations were made by re-
presentatives of the civil concerns. It was agreed
that the most important cause of the poor results was
the shortage of labour, and this question was thoroughly
discussed. Various remedial suggestions were made, such
as the employment of Korean and Chinese labour and the
sanctioning of juvenile labour in the pits. It is
expected that measures will be taken by both the Com-
merce Department and the Public Welfare Department to
mitigate the labour shortage in gold mines in order to
facilitate the realization of the increased gold pro-
duction plan.

=== Page 379 === (confidence 0.89)

Attachment No. 3a
SALE OF AMERICAN GOLD CONCESSION IN CHOSEN.
(From THE JAPAN ADVERTISER, August 24, 1939)
MINE NEGOTIATIONS CAUSING DISCUSSION
Reports Nihon hogyo Buying U.S. Gold Concession in Korea
Aids Share Prices
Traders Said Cautious
Japanese Concern Reportedly Planning 25,000,000
5-Year Expansion Plan
The tentative agreement for the sale of America's
only gold mine in the Japanese Empire to Japanese mining
interests has stirred up a deal of comment in the
foreign press and more than passing changes in the
price of the gold mine's shares on the London exchange.
(There seems to be no regular listing of the issue on
Wall Street.)
For some time the shares of the American Oriental
Consolidated Mining Company, which owns what has been
termed the richest gold mine in the Orient - at Unzan
in Korea - had been gradually sinking. Mining had been
profitable, but because of limitations on the export of
money, a bank balance of ¥4,500,000 gradually had been
building up in Japan. Shareholders, with little
prospect of receiving dividends of any size for some
time to come, were inclined to sell, but there were
few buyers on the market.
By early July the price of the share, whose face
value is $10, had fallen to about $3.50. Evidently
rumors of the success of the negotiations, which had
been going on for two years between officials of the
American mining company, and the Nihon Kogyo Kabushiki
Kaisha, a subsidiary of the Manchuria Industrial Develop-
ment Corporation and operator of almost all of the im-
portant gold mines in Japan, began reaching England and
the United States during the early part of the month.
The share began rising. By July 17, the price of the
gold shares had risen from $3.50 to approximately $6.50.
The actual news of the announcement of the success of
the negotiations on about July 18 caused a comparatively
small rise to $7. Since then the shares have risen but
gradually, being quoted at approximately $8 in London
on August 4.
London Comment
The London comment on the sale of the mine and the
price of the shares has centered in the fact that the
$8,174,500 offered for the mine figures out at roughly
¥19

=== Page 381 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
*l9 a share. Yet traders have been willing to pay but
$7 to $8 dollars a share.
Commenting on the small rise (50 cents) in the
price of the shares which accompanied the announcement
that an agreement on the sale had been reached, the
London Financial Times comments:
"If an agreement is promptly concluded and the
initial $2,270,000 is actually paid over next month
(August 31) in cash and short-term bills then the
market will no doubt abandon its present cautious
attitude. Such a development would mean that the
Oriental Consolidated would have over Il ($4.68) per
share cash in hand. The balance of 10s ($2.34) in the
current quotation of the shares ($7.00) would be very
little to pay for the prospect of receiving at least
a further 13 ($14.05) per share over the next four years.
"The market could not shake off a suspicion ***
that either the Japanese Government is unduly generous,
or that something will happen to prevent the full terms
being secured. Actually this caution may well be ill-
founded. The Oriental Consolidated's properties are
very valuable - the proved ore reserves in half-a-dozen
mines alone contain over $5,000,000 worth of gold - and
Japan is not only hungry for ali the gold it can get,
but is very jealous of foreign ownership of any of its
important enterprises. At the same time Japan would
not wish to antagonise the United States authorities
by attempting to enforce ungenerous terms on the American
controllers of the Oriental Consolidated."
Agreement
The tentative sales agreement was out between
Mr. Lewis Henry, president of the American company,
and officials of the Nihon Kogyo Kabushiki Kaisha,
during a visit to Tokyo made by Mr. Henry in June,
and tentatively approved on July 14 after the permis-
sion of the Finance Ministry for the export of the
money had been secured.
A first installment of ¥2,270,000 will be paid by
August 31 (one week from today). 0f this, $756,700
will be in cash, $756,700 in a 30-day sight draft under
a letter of credit of the Yokohama Specie Bank, Limited,
New York City, and $756,600 in a 60-day sight draft
under the same letter of credit. Another $4,524,000
is payable by August 31, 1942, and the final $1,362, 500
by August 31, 1943.
The Yokohama Specie Bank will guarantee payment of
all deferred amounts, together with 4 per cent interest.
The Nihon Kogyo Kabushiki Kaisha is scheduled to
begin operation of the American mines at Unzan on
September l. Immediately on taking over, it is under-
stood, the concern plans to put into effect a five-year
plan for production increase. Some ¥25,000,000, it is
estimated

=== Page 382 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
estimated, will be spent on this plan.
Last Concession
The Unzan mine has remained as the last of those
foreign mining concessions which Korea granted to for-
eign engineering companies in the pre-Japanese period
of its history. There were at one time several others,
two British concessions, a French concession, and a
German concession, as well as a one or more Japanese
concessions in the country. According to some Japanese
reports the German mines were taken over during the
world war, according to others they had been already
sold to Japanese interests when the war broke out one
British concession, changed hands several times and at
last proved so unprofitable (having been virtually
worked out) that it was given up as worthless by the
operators. It is understood that later the mining
rights were sold to a Japanese concern which opened a
few new small mines on the property.
The French gold mining concession was sold to the
Nihon Kogyo early last year. The Japanese operations
were in copper mining, and it is understood that they
are still being operated though at present they hardly
come under the category of concessions.
The Oriental Mining Company's territory at Unzan,
the only significantly profitable mining concession of
the group, was granted to a Mr. James R. Morse by the
King, afterwards Emperor, of Korea on July 2, 1895.
The concession gave Mr. Morse the mining rights
to the whole province of Unzan, some 600 square miles
in all, as well as the water power and timbering rights
in the area. In the intervening 40 years practically
all of the timber has been cut down.
Later the concession, which came into the hands
of the Oriental Mining Company, was renewed several
times, and at the time Korea came under Japanese control
the concession was scheduled to expire in 44 years (1954).
The concession has not been renewed since that time.
For the past 15 years the production of the mine
has been somewhat steady (up to the time when the
announcement of figures was banned) at approximately
1,500,000 grams of gold and 1,000,000 grams of silver
a year.
But the operation of the mines has become more and
more unprofitable as the shafts have gone deeper and
deeper. Some 10 years ago the main shaft was 1,400 feet
deep, and 1,82l miners were required for the mining. At
present, the main mine is more than 3,000 feet deep and
4,500 miners are required for approximately the same
gold production. There are 30 foreign mining engineers
working at the mine, the bulk of the actual miners being
Koreans.
At

=== Page 383 === (confidence 0.96)
-4 -
At the same time, the quality of the ore is said
by mining men to be rather poor, being listed at
approximately 9.2 grams per ton.

SALE OF AMERICAN GOLD CONCESSTON IN CHOSEN
(From TIME, September 11, 1939)
CHOSEN GOLD
In the So4th Yeur znd 5th Moon, 10th Day (July 2,

away the mineral rights to Goo square m: les of the

was imprisoned, his wife assassinated by a Japanese-

By that time, too, Promoter Hunt, a former presi-

Oriental Consolidated Mining Co. witn the aid of Brit-

When Japan stopped fooling in 1910, annexed

Oriental Consolidated was te oldest, biggest, richest,

served the provisions of Oriental's charter: for pay-

Government, the mining company was free from all
taxes, import-export duties. Ei ht years ago Japan
got tough, embargoed cold exports, forced oriental to
sell gold to her at prices below the world merket,
paid off in unsteady Yen. Last week Oriental, last
big U. S. concession in Korea, got out while the going
was still passable.

President Lewis Henry, of Elmira, N. Y., sold his

Co., Ltd.). The deal: $756,700 cash; two payments of
$756,700 within 30 and 60 days respectively; 4,542,000
payable by Aug. 31, 1942; $1,362,500 payable by Aug. 31,
1943. All deferred payments were guaranteed by the
Yokohama Specie Bank, Ltd., New York City, with in-
terest at 4%.

GOLD MINE IIN KOREA SOLD BY AMERICANS
(From THE JAPAI: ADVERT1SER, July 29, 1939)
Japanese buy Unzar Property, Terminating Holdings by
Foreign interesta
The Japan Mining Company of Tokyo has purchased
the famous Unzan gold mine in North ieian Irovince,
Korea, from the Oriental Corsolidated Minin Company
of New York, terminating all gold opeca:ions by Ameri-
cans in Korea, according to Domei.
Because the operation and management of gold mines
in Japanese territory by foreigners were considered
disadvantageous to this Country's gold policy, the
Korean Government-General reportedly induced the Japan
Mining Company, the largest gold mining firm in this
country, to negotiate with the American concern for the
purchase of the Unzan mine.
The only difficulty hampering the conclusion of
the deal was the matter of exchange control. As a
result of negotiations between the Government-General
and the Finance Ministry, however, the matter is now
said to have been solved and the final coniract for
the purchase is said to have been signed.
The purchase price is kept secret, but Domei
reports that it is estimated to be in the vicinity of
¥25,000,000, to be paid in installments extending over
a period of five years.

Attachment 4a
GOLD FROM SEABED OFF KOREAN COAST
(From THE JAPAN ADVERTISER, July 15, 1939)
GOLD TO BE MINED OFF KOREAN COAST
Test Prospecting in Seabed Near Moppo Yields
Satisfactory Results
Firm Set Up for Venture
New Method Will Use Embankments,
Take Advantage of Shifts
in Tides
The Korean Physico-Chemical Company under the
presidency of Dr. Masatoshi Okochi, director of the
Institute for Physical and Chemical Research here, is
now completing plans for the mining of gold from the
seabed off some islands near Moppo in southern Korea,
reports the Hochi.
Test prospecting done by experts sent from
Dr. Okochi's institute in February this year is said
to have yielded very satisfactory results. Nuggets
of pure gold weighing about half an ounce were obtained
from the site of the proposed mining operations.
The Korean Physico-Chemical Mining Company,
capitalized at 3,000,000, was established for this
seabed gold mining this April. It is thought that the
preparations for the actual operations may be completed
by October.
The firm will use a new method for its digging, it
was disclosed. The method, worked out by the institute's
staff, will have embankments built around the spot to be
worked, and will take advantage of the tides to have
the seabed drained a little. The remaining muddy water
will be removed by suction pumps, before small-size
dredgers are put to work, scooping up the gold-bearing
sod.
Present plans call for the building of an embank-
ment inolosing about 416 acres. When the work is in
full swing, it is thought that more than 40,000 acres
will be under exploitation.
When the Moppo region has been exhausted of its
gold, the company plans to turn its attention to the
seabed off the North and South Heian Provinces in
northwestern Korea.
The

=== Page 390 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
The Korea Government-General has already granted
permission to the company to use the land reclaimed by
the embankments for cotton plantations.
Dr. Okochi has just returned from a two-week's
inspection trip to Moppo. He admitted that he had
picked Moppo for the scene of his mining operation in
the first place because he had heard legends about gold
coming from the sea off Moppo and that because he had
followed up those legends by doin a little investiga-
tion of his own some years ago as to the possibilities
of the presence of gold there.
The director of the institute declared that Japan
is not a have-not country as most people presume.
Japan has the resources, but lacks the means of fully
exploiting them. It is more a case of poverty of
methods than a poverty of resources, he stated. Korea
he cited as likely to have gold over one third of its
area.
(From THE JAPAN TIMES, July 15, 1939)
PREPARATORY WORK FOR DREDGING GOLD
FROM CHOSEN SEA BOTTOM STARTED
(Tokyo, Friday, July 14, 1939)
Work to drain away the sea water off Moppo at the
southwestern edge of Chosen to exploit ¥1,500,000,000
worth of alluvial gold, which is believed to be buried
under the sea bottom there, is now getting under way by
the Chosen Physico-Chemical Mining Company, established
on April 20 this year capitalized at ¥3,000,000, with
Viscount Dr. Masatoshi Okochi, President of the Physico-
Chemical Research Institute, as its head, the Hochi reports.
The sea faced by Moppo is dotted with numerous small
islands including the Island of Chin, for which it is
called the "Aegean Sea of Chosen", and a tradition has
it that a large quantity of alluvial gold is buried there.
The tradition was proved to be true by a group of
investigators of the Physico-Chemical Research Institute
who succeeded in obtaining some grains of genuine gold
from alluvion in the so-called Chosen Aegean Sea as a
result of trial operation last February.
According to plans of the Chosen Physico-Chemical
Mining Company, a long pier will be constructed to drain
the water off and thereby to change the area into swamps.
Next the muddy water in the area will be pumped off by
special pumps developed by the company. After surface
soils are removed by another newly-invented pumps,
dredgers

=== Page 391 === (confidence 0.99)
-3 -
dredgers of small size will be used to dig for alluvial
gola.
It is further planned that the area will be used
for cultivation of cotton after gold deposits are com-
pletely exploited.
As a first step toward beginning the mining opera-
tion, a dike is being constructed around an area of
500,O00-tsubo in the sea by the Chosen rhysico-Chemical
Mining Company.
Viscount Dr. Masatoshi Okochi, who returned here
Wednesday from a two-week inspection tour of the spot
off Moppo, said to a Hochi reporter:
"I will go through with this undertaking because
of the successful result of the trial digging. It is
said that Japan belongs to the category of "have-nots",
but I dare to say Japan is a country of "haves".
"It is quite possible for us to obtain immeasurable
raw materials everywhere in the country if we make use
of our knowledge of science. We should think of lack
of methods of obtaining resources instead of complain-
ing lack of resources. As for Chosen, about one-third
of the total area is divided into mine-lot of alluvial
gold. I intend to start gold mining operation in other
districts there when we finish the work off Moppo."

Attachment 4b
GOLD SIFTER INVENTION
(From THE JAPAN ADVERTISER, Ju1y 14, 1939)
Officials Inspect Sifter for Gold
New Invention for Placer Mining
Claimed Able to Work Poor Deposits
High technical experts fron the Finance Mini:try
recently visi ted Mr. Shozapuro  mura' workshop near
the Tokyo-Kanagawa prefectural border to inspect his
device for sifting gold from sand, reports the  iyako.
Mr. Kimura claims that his invention can separate
one part of gold from a million parts of sand with
profit, whereas it had been unprofitable to work de-
posits with less than eight parts of gold to a million
parts of sand.
The new device has a pump and two hoses. It
draws in the sand thr ugh one hose and by stirrin the
sand within the machine, manages to separate the old
particles fron the sand through the difference in
their specific gravities.
In the demonstration before the officials fron.
the Finance Ministry, Mr. Kimura mixed in some gold
into a lot of sand, and before an :hour was up, the
machine successfully sifted the gold from the sand.
Officials are withholding their opinion on the
invention, and in the meanwhile will go to the Kuji
River in Ibaraki Prefecture to see the machine pick
up gold from the river bed.
The inventor, who works in a colliery company,
says that most Japanese rivers have gold in a ratio
of about seven or less parts of gold to a million
parts of sand and mud. If this machine is put into
widespread use, it is claimed that the country will
be the richer by several billion yen.
The inventor declares that he is more interested
in getting official support for the widespread use of
his machine than in working the river beds himself
for his personal profit. Besides, he says, with the
present control of materials, he would find it ex-
tremely difficult to manufacture these devices on a
large scale.
Mr. Kimura has been studying gold mining for
the last 20 years, and completed his apparatus about
a year ago.

GOLD PRICE
(THE JAPAN CHRONICLE, September 8, 1939)
EFFECTS ON GOLD PRICE
Finance Department Planning No Change in uotation Now
On the basis of the British governrient's announced
figure for the stabilization of the Anglo-t. S. cross
rate, namely above $4, and the old quotetion of 168
shillings per ounce published the same day it is pointed
out that the gold purchasing price in Japan should be
about ¥17.30 per momme as against the present quota-
tion of ¥14.43.
Certain quarters, arguing on the above lines, are
urging the authorities to take steps to adjust the gap
but the authorities of the Banis of Japan contend that
1t would be premature to raise the old purchase price
from the standpoint of Japan's low-price policy and
also due to the difficulty of forecastin the future
developments of the war in Europe.
On the other hand it is empnasized that there is
a danger of increased gold smuggling resultant from
the wide price difference and that the future tendency
of gold production will require careful watching.
For the time being, however the authorities of
the Finance Ministry have announced that they have no
intention of altering the gold purchase price.
